#158. Barnsley workers strike over nursery privatisation by Reform council

Women say move will be ‘massively detrimental’ to services for vulnerable children, and hit their own pensions

The 33 women waving banners outside a nursery in the South Yorkshire village of Grimethorpe have never been on strike before.

Passing cars beep their horns in support of the workers, who have taken action at six nurseries run by Barnsley council after its Reform UK leadership agreed to transfer them to private companies. They say the move will have a hugely negative impact on the services they provide to vulnerable children, and to their own pensions.

Such is the strength of feeling among the women, who are members of the GMB union, that turnout at their strike ballot was 100%, with every single member backing the walkout.

“For low-paid working women to take a stand is significant,” said Victoria Austin, a GMB regional organiser, adding that the work done by the nursery staff was above and beyond and “the council should be singing that from the rooftops”.

Instead, the council made the “massively detrimental” decision to push ahead with privatising the nurseries, only telling the workers about the impact on their pensions 36 hours before the transfer was due to take place, Austin said.

While the plans began under the previous Labour administration, councillors at the time had reassured nursery workers that their pay and benefits, including pensions, would stay the same.

Austin said Reform, which took over the council at the local elections in May after half a century of Labour leadership, was “not interested” in the workers’ fight, and was “wanting to build a legacy around balancing the books”.

Some local people see the transfer of nursery ownership as an example of heavy-handed slashing by Reform councils, which has also included closing care homes and cutting social care.

“As an elected council, their priority should be not letting children get left behind and doing the right thing by communities,” Austin said.

The workers and the union feel the transfer has been handled badly. One nursery is shuttered after the council deregistered it with Ofsted to allow the private provider to register, but it then pulled out.

The council said there was no legal duty for councils to run nursery services and the change would “reduce the amount of council tax payers’ money needed to support the nurseries”.

The council said after receiving legal advice that the staff were no longer eligible for the local government pension scheme before the transfer, “that information was shared quickly with staff and trade unions”.

William Brown, the leader of Barnsley council, said: “We’re disappointed that GMB members have voted in favour of strike action. The decision to transfer these nurseries to another provider was taken to protect jobs and retain these services for families.”

He said additional provision had been made for families on the three strike days and the council was continuing to engage with the union.

For Kimberley Darlington, Emma Jones, Lucy Kolconko and Charlotte Hawe, who work at Darfield nursery, the strike was not just about the plans to move them to a lower-value pension but to protect the families they supported.

Hawe said: “We’re often the first point of contact. I’ve arranged lots of food parcels for families who need them.”

Kolconko has taken families to visit the doctor when they have needed to. Darlington added: “We went out to a house to show a parent how to play with her Send (special educational needs and disabilities) children, she just didn’t know.”

The nursery workers have taught a three-year-old child with special educational needs to walk, which the parent had not done, and helped another family who were living with a rat infestation and mouldy walls to get help from their landlord.

They believe these are things they would be unable to do in a private setting where staff may be responsible for more children. In Barnsley, the nursery workers each look after four children over the age of two, while in private settings this can be five children.

“We just won’t have time to do these things. We’ve done all this work and the council has turned on us,” said Kolconko, a senior nursery worker.

#156. Air pollution soars as fog traps emissions in England’s urban areas

Particles from fireworks and bonfires likely to have contributed to levels well above WHO’s daily guideline

Air pollution soared to “very high” levels across urban parts of England on Monday as still, foggy weather kept emissions trapped after a weekend of fireworks and bonfires.

Government monitoring showed 11 sites, mainly in London and Greater Manchester, reached the highest band on its 10-point index for particulate matter in the 24 hours to 10am. A further 27 sites recorded “high” levels across the capital, the north-west, the Midlands, Yorkshire and the east of England.

The worst reading was in Hillingdon, west London, where fine particles (PM2.5) peaked at a 24-hour average of 129 micrograms per cubic metre, more than eight times the World Health Organization’s daily guideline. In the north-west, Manchester Piccadilly, Wigan, Blackburn, Preston and Rochdale all reached the “very high” band.

Particulate matter consists of tiny solid particles and liquid droplets suspended in the air. Exposure is linked to heart and lung disease and cancer.

Jane Burston, the CEO of the Clean Air Fund, said although monitoring networks could not determine where pollution came from, it was highly likely the pollution was from autumn celebrations.

“It’s a combination of the weather, which has been foggy and not windy at all, and then the fireworks and bonfires and Diwali,” she said. “What it means is the wind doesn’t blow the pollution away and the fog keeps it trapped down in the same place.

“If you’ve got asthma or a lung condition, you absolutely will not have wanted to go outside this morning or yesterday – it can bring on asthma attacks, coughing and wheezing.

“But for everybody, it’s dangerous. People in some of the worst-affected areas are breathing particulate matter concentrations five to 10 times above normal annual levels,” she added.

Burston called for better pollution forecasts, more widely accessible health alerts and stronger action to reduce emissions from wood burning and transport. “If we had air quality at a much safer lower level all year round, then on nights like Bonfire Night, the extra pollution is less likely to push us to extreme levels,” she said.

Larissa Lockwood, the director of campaigns at Global Action Plan, said the high pollution levels were “not surprising at a time of year when people are lighting log burners, having bonfires and lighting fireworks. On top of the normal levels from transport and industry, we see higher levels of air pollution and they’re then unable to disperse because they’re trapped by the fog.”

Domestic burning and transport are the biggest sources of particulate pollution, she said. Lockwood called for a public health campaign on air pollution and “burn alerts”, asking people in towns and cities not to use wood burners when levels are high.

“We need to phase out non-essential burning in our towns and cities so that we don’t experience these unhealthy air pollution levels,” she added.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

A spokesperson for the mayor of London said: “On the advice of forecasters at Imperial College London, we’ve issued a high air pollution alert for Tuesday 10 November. Monitoring data show that pollution levels rose sharply across London from Friday to Sunday and remained elevated into Monday morning.”

A Clean Air Greater Manchester spokesperson said: “Higher particulate pollution levels occur each year around Bonfire Night, when fireworks and bonfires add particulate matter to the air. This year, weather conditions are also an important factor, with light winds and fog creating conditions in which pollution from a range of sources can accumulate near ground level. The latest information and health advice provided by Defra is available online.”

A Defra spokesperson said: “The current still weather conditions are leading to high air pollution levels in parts of the country. We encourage people to check the latest information for their area and follow the accompanying health advice, particularly those who may be vulnerable to the effects of air pollution.

“We are continuing to closely monitor the situation and expect levels to fall later in the week as winds pick up.”

#154. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#151. Jemima Goldsmith says ex-partner Russell Brand physically assaulted her

TV producer, who was in relationship with ex-comedian that reportedly ended in 2014, accuses him of ‘violence, coercive control and abuse’

Russell Brand sentenced to more than 10 years in prison for rape and sexual assault

Jemima Goldsmith has said Russell Brand physically assaulted her and she accused him of “violence, coercive control and abuse”.

The 52-year-old TV producer said she has always been “strongly and unequivocally” on the side of Brand’s victims as she opened up about her own “painful” experience with her former partner in a social media post on Monday.

Shortly after Brand was sentenced to more than 10 years in prison, Goldsmith posted on X: “I am in awe of the courageous women who came forward at Brand’s trial, told their stories, testified and ensured justice was done.

“I have had my own personal experience of his violence, coercive control and abuse. I had to call the police to have him removed from my house in 2014 during a violent incident. I chose not to press charges at the time and not to go public with this, as I was worried about the impact of the press attention on my then teenage children.

“I have neither seen nor spoken to Brand since that period in 2014. Whilst my pain in relation to this pales in comparison to what the women have testified in court have suffered, this was a difficult period in my life which has had a lasting impact.”

On Thursday, Brand was found guilty of raping a fan and sexually assaulting three women in separate incidents between 2004 and 2009. He was cleared of two further charges of rape and one of attempted indecent assault.

Goldsmith, known professionally as Jemima Khan, is reported to have been in a relationship with Brand for a year from around September 2013. On X, Goldsmith said she had assisted the journalists who had exposed Brand in a Channel 4 Dispatches documentary and in reports in the Times and Sunday Times in 2023.

“I also undertook to testify in court to his abusiveness, if the newspaper that published the victims’ accounts were ever to be sued,” she said.

Goldsmith made the comments in light of renewed press reporting about a harassment injunction that she and Brand had taken out against a woman named Szilvia Berki in 2014.

Goldsmith, who married former Pakistan cricket captain Imran Khan in 1995, said the online commentary and media reporting about the injunction had presented “an untrue narrative” and opened her up to “vile hatred and abuse online”.

Berki had made allegations against Brand to the police after she had been booked to give him a massage at Goldsmith’s home in June 2014. It is understood the police found there was no case to answer.

“I was present the whole time he was with her, and I did not see anything untoward, despite the fact that he was later found guilty of sexually assaulting other women and he later physically assaulted me. If I saw any man abuse any woman under any circumstances, I would do my utmost to stop them, without hesitation,” Goldsmith said in her statement.

Goldsmith alleged that a series of “untrue stories” appeared in the tabloid press and that Berki embarked on a campaign of “threatening abuse online and in emails”. The Guardian has not been able to reach Berki.

Brand and Goldsmith applied for a harassment injunction against Berki.

The injunction was granted by the high court judge and former leading counsel in the Leveson inquiry Mr Justice Jay. It was brought to light by the non-profit campaigning thinktank Tax Policy Associates, founded by the former tax lawyer Dan Neidle, after Brand’s conviction.

This article was amended on 9 November 2026 to clarify the circumstances of the injunction.

#150. Tommy Walsh, co-presenter of BBC’s Ground Force, dies aged 69

Former builder got his big break after restoring the home of a producer who was working on a precursor to the show

Tommy Walsh, the building and DIY expert who appeared on television shows including Ground Force, has died aged 69 after a long illness.

Walsh started his career as a professional builder before rising to fame alongside Alan Titchmarsh and Charlie Dimmock on the popular BBC garden makeover show from 1997 to 2005.

He went on to front programmes including Clean It, Fix It and Cowboys and Angels and joined the BBC show Homes Under the Hammer in 2021, following renovation projects and attending property auctions.

Walsh’s agent, Debbie Schiesser, told the Press Association: “Tommy died peacefully after a long illness surrounded by his family.

Walsh, from Hackney in east London, ran his own property business before his television career. He got his big break after restoring the house of a BBC producer who was working on ideas for a show that would become Ground Force.

He told the BBC in 2004: “She asked me to look at a couple of pilots. One of them was the precursor to Ground Force, called Over the Garden Wall.

“It was like watching paint dry. It was terrible.”

Walsh turned down the chance to star on the show three times, but agreed when his eight-year-old daughter told him she had made a £10 bet with a boy at school that her dad was going to be on TV.

At its peak, Ground Force attracted 12 million viewers. In one episode, the team was flown out to South Africa to work in secret on Nelson Mandela’s garden, building a pergola and water feature.

Walsh attended Parmiter’s grammar school in Bethnal Green, east London, and played football for the Old Parmiterians.

The club wrote on X: “It is with great sadness that we announce the passing of Tommy Walsh. A Parmiters boy he played for the club for many years in goal but made a great career as a TV personality in shows such as Ground Force. He will be sorely missed. RIP Tommy.”

He was married to Marie Walsh and was a father to three children: Charlotte, Natalie and Jonjo.

#148. Headteachers consider leaving jobs earlier than planned after new Ofsted inspection

Almost two-thirds of school leaders polled say new regime is much more intense than previous framework

More than half of headteachers in England who were polled after their schools were inspected under Ofsted’s new regime are considering cutting short their careers as a result of the experience.

In response to the question “Has the inspection affected how long you intend to stay in your role?”, 52% of those who responded said they were thinking of leaving their job sooner than planned.

Amost two-thirds (64%) said the new inspections were “much more intense” than under the previous framework, while nearly nine in 10 (86%) struggled with their mental health and wellbeing while inspectors were on site.

The findings from the survey by the National Association of Head Teachers (NAHT) are a damning indictment of the new regime that is the result of a radical overhaul after the suicide of the primary school headteacher Ruth Perry in January 2023.

The changes, which were introduced a year ago, were intended to reduce the strain and workload caused by the inspection process, but many school leaders say they have made things worse.

One primary headteacher who took part in the survey said: “Not sure my physical or mental health could cope with another inspection like this framework. Have been a head for 15 years across three schools and have never felt less able to do my job than I did during the inspection.”

A deputy head at an all-through school said: “I previously had aspirations for headship. I will now never go for headship. The stress and anxiety of the inspection has ended that dream … this is the most challenging Ofsted I have ever completed in a career of over 20 years.”

A primary school chief executive said: “I’m very tired of the high stakes, the stress, the worry that comes from the whole process. It’s on a different level.”

A headteacher at a secondary special school said: “I will not do another inspection as head. I will leave before the next one – it made too much of an impact on my personal life – I didn’t see my children for months on the run up to going into window.”

The general secretary of the NAHT, Paul Whiteman, said the survey findings showed “the unmitigated failure of an unaccountable and out of control inspectorate” and said Ofsted’s actions were driving experienced leaders from schools.

The education secretary, Lucy Powell, has indicated she is looking at further changes to Ofsted, but Whiteman said that “another belated rearranging of the deckchairs which sidelines the profession is wholly insufficient”.

The survey, which had 242 responses between June and October, comes after teaching unions declared no confidence in the schools watchdog and threatened strike ballots unless significant wellbeing issues caused by the new inspection framework were fixed.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

An Ofsted spokesperson said many leaders had found inspections helpful and constructive over the last year, but acknowledged that others had highlighted the impact on their workload and wellbeing.

“That’s why we’re working with the secretary of state on ways to reduce the pressure felt by heads and teachers, without losing our shared focus on the best interests of children and their families,” the spokesperson said.

A Department for Education spokesperson said teachers do extraordinary work and inspections should support improvement without adding to pressures already on schools.

“That’s why we are working with Ofsted and school leaders to ensure accountability is collaborative, fair, proportionate and focused on helping every school get better, so every child gets the education they deserve.”

In the US, you can call or text the 988 Suicide & Crisis Lifeline at 988 or chat at 988lifeline.org. In Australia, the crisis support service Lifeline is 13 11 14. Other international helplines can be found at befrienders.org

#145. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#143. What Marco Rubio’s sidestepping of New Delhi says about the US’s stance on India

Rubio’s decision not to meet Narendra Modi last week was seen by some as a snub – but by focusing on business leaders and oil, the secretary of state made American priorities clear. Plus: the artist bringing a female perspective to men’s clothing

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

When the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, visited India for the second time this year, headlines concentrated on who he met and where he went.

The fact that Rubio bypassed New Delhi and did not meet the prime minister, Narendra Modi, during his trip last week, and instead traveled to the financial capital, Mumbai, where he met India’s foreign minister, Subrahmanyam Jaishankar (pictured top with Rubio), and business leaders, did not go unnoticed. Nor did his decision to travel to Jamnagar, the small Gujarati town that is home to Reliance Industries’ colossal oil refinery complex.

Bilateral ties between the two countries have come under strain under the second Trump administration, particularly over tariffs and India’s purchase of Russian oil. Yet, Rubio’s return to India just five months after his first visit as secretary of state signals that the country remains useful for president Donald Trump’s ambitions after all.

As director of the South Asia Program at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace, Milan Vaishnav has spent a decade tracking India’s place in the world, particularly its ties with the US. Vaishnav has described Trump 2.0 as “unbound” marking a new era in India-US relations.

Tracking foreign affairs, he told me, is a minefield right now. From the White House’s transactional approach to global alliances, ongoing conflicts in the Middle East and consequential elections around the world, politics is changing at a pace that means all predictions are off the table. “I think anyone who tells you they know exactly what is going on and how it fits into their neat theory of the world is lying to you,” says Vaishnav.

I asked him about the many confusing aspects of the US-India relationship, from the hostile political rhetoric to the US embrace of Pakistan, as well as Rubio’s latest visit.

Ahead of Rubio’s visit, several commentators noted the secretary of state’s “unusual” itinerary. Vaishnav thought the pre-visit chatter about Rubio skipping Delhi to visit Mumbai and Jamnagar was overblown. “Not every bilateral meeting has to take place in a national capital,” he says.

But that doesn’t mean it wasn’t noteworthy. “I do think the choice of venues was intentional. This administration and, particularly, this president, sees foreign policy through the lens of ‘deal-making’. And if you’re trying to drum up business deals, what better place to visit than Mumbai?” he says.

The Jamnagar visit, where Rubio met Mukesh Ambani, the chair of Reliance, also indicates some politicking. “At a time when petrol prices are extremely high in the United States and are likely to be a major issue in the 3 November midterm elections, the administration wants to show that it is doing everything it can to bring them down. India is a major consumer of oil, but it is also a major exporter of refined petroleum products,” says Vaishnav. “One of Rubio’s objectives was to encourage Reliance and, by extension, India to process more Venezuelan crude [oil],” he added.

While this could present an opportunity for India to advance relations, it’s not a given: the US president remains, after all – and to put it lightly – mercurial.

“The biggest plot twist for me has been the Trump administration’s embrace of Pakistan after the May 2025 conflict with India,” says Vaishnav. Pakistan’s standing in Washington had eroded over the years, especially under Joe Biden, who left office without ever having made a presidential phone call to his counterpart in Pakistan. “Many officials in Congress had grown exhausted with Pakistan’s double game, speaking the language of partnership on the one hand while undermining US interests on the other,” says Vaishnav.

But, after coming to power the second time, Trump thanked the country for its help in nabbing a suspect linked to a 2021 bombing in Afghanistan that killed 13 US service members. This reversal in Pakistan’s fortunes gathered pace after the May conflict with India, which Trump took credit for ending. In June, he welcomed Asim Munir, Pakistan’s army chief, to the White House, after Munir called for the president to be nominated for the Nobel peace prize.

“I chalk up the reversal to a few factors: Trump’s well-known strongman envy, Pakistan’s savvy diplomacy, and the prospect of energy and crypto deals involving members of Trump’s inner circle,” says Vaishnav. For him, it isyet another demonstration of how much weight Trump places on deals, transactions and short-term wins. “For New Delhi, this means Modi’s personal rapport with Trump and 25 years of investment in the bilateral relationship offer no guarantee against a 180-degree reversal in US policy,” he adds.

In a recent piece, Vaishnav’s colleague Sameer Lalwani pointed out that the US relationship with India remains more resilient than the political rhetoric may suggest with quiet, but nonetheless strategic, progress being made.

As Lalwani points out, bilateral trade reached a record high between India and the US in 2025; nearly $68bn worth of AI infrastructure investment in India has been pledged by US technology companies; and the two countries have carried out record number of joint military exercises this year.

Despite these wins, in terms of future bilateral relations, “there are at least three reasons for caution,” warns Vaishnav. First, he points to the onslaught of US actions that have dented Indian interests. Trump had imposed a punitive 50% tariff on India in 2025, which was reduced to 18% earlier this year, but without a comprehensive trade deal being reached. Second, on immigration, the White House’s H-1B visa and green card restrictions have disproportionately affected Indian workers in Silicon Valley.

“This has created a real trust deficit on the Indian side, and with good reason,” says Vaishnav. The frustration was on display earlier this month, when New Delhi branded remarks made by the US vice-president, JD Vance, about Indian professionals as “deeply offensive”. Furthermore, Vaishnav argues that while governments and private sector firms might be great at making announcements, they’re not always a guarantee of delivery.

His final caution is how political tensions can have a chilling effect. The trade negotiations offer a telling example: in January, the US commerce secretary, Howard Lutnick, blamed the lack of a trade deal on Modi’s failure to phone Trump, a characterisation that New Delhi has disputed. Vaishnav says: “Much of the progress we are seeing is the product of decisions made years ago. It is one thing to keep existing projects moving, but turbulence in the relationship could make new initiatives a heavier lift.”

If India is serious about making its ambition to be a global powerhouse a reality, Vaishnav thinks there’s a clearcut way to win. “I would argue that the most important investment is political – ensuring India remains a robust, inclusive, participatory democracy,” he says. “On the economic front, India has made massive new investments in physical and digital infrastructure. But the returns on those investments will be limited unless the government doubles down on reforms: more efficient dispute resolution, an impartial legal system, even-handed tax enforcementand regulatory certainty.”

Ultimately, neither side can afford to treat the other as expendable. The test now is whether strategic foundations can hold at a time of global political upheaval.

Vadra, the son of the Congress leader, Priyanka Gandhi, defended his work and also refused a request to remove images featuring the national flag. In a press statement, the venue said certain photos were “inconsistent” with its policy.

Air pollution | Emergency measures, including a ban on coal-fired tandoors in restaurants and restrictions on diesel generators, have come into effect in Delhi, as air quality dropped to the poor category at the onset of the annual pollution season.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

Film-maker Ritu Sarin, director of the annual Dharamshala international film festival, which runs from 29 October to 1 November, suggests a film from this year’s lineup:

“Shot over three years on real locations, Anmol Sidhu’s sophomore feature, Flowers of Acacia, follows wedding orchestra dancers in rural Punjab with a gaze that allows all the large brutalities and smaller intimacies to shine through. Sidhu’s confident, consummate grasp of the medium is on full display in terrifying scenes that stretch to the edge of what is palatable. The camera refuses to look away.”

That’s the number of trees Mumbai’s municipal authorities authorised to be felled over five months this year, to make way for development works, according to the Indian Express. Of these, about 3,500 will be permanently removed, while the rest will be replanted elsewhere.

Prarthna Singh has consistently placed the female identity at the heart of her artistic practice, documenting Indian women’s aspirations and acts of rebellion in male-dominated spaces. When commissioned to photograph rkivecity’s menswear line, made from discarded clothes reworked into new garments, she turned that same gaze outward. The setting was the acclaimed 151-year-old Mayo College boys’ school in Ajmer, Rajasthan – Singh attended the linked girls’ school, and credits it with shaping her rebellious spirit.

“I was thinking about how I could bring a sense of intimacy and camaraderie of boarding-school life to the images, because that exists,” she told me. One image shows a pair of hands holding another boy’s jacket, the faces left out of the frame, so the garment stands in for the familiarity they share. Seeing this work against the backdrop of students taking to the streets to fight for their rights lends the series a lasting significance. “This was just my way of showing all of this is connected. These worlds are not existing separately,” said Singh.

#140. Tens of thousands protest against Narendra Modi regime in Delhi

Led by Congress party, India’s opposition alliance channels anger over removal of 130 million names from voter roll

Thousands of opposition supporters have gathered in Delhi as protests against the Narendra Modi government continue to gather momentum.

Led by the Congress party, India’s opposition alliance has brought together supporters from across the country, amid growing anger over a large-scale revision of the country’s voter roll that has seen 130 million names erased.

Opposition leaders have alleged the process gives Modi’s ruling Bharatiya Janata party (BJP) an electoral advantage, and have called for the resignation and impeachment of the chief election commissioner, Gyanesh Kumar. The BJP has denied the claims.

Addressing tens of thousands of party workers and supporters, the opposition leader Rahul Gandhi said Kumar had “carried out the biggest crime. He has tried to silence the voice of people, strangulate our country.”

Gandhi said the opposition, united under the acronym India (Indian National Developmental Inclusive Alliance), would not let “democracy be finished in the country” and warned Modi: “You can’t run anywhere.”

The protest was one of the largest shows of opposition strength in years, taking place amid a wider resurgence of anti-government dissent which has been systematically crushed over the 12 years of the Modi regime.

The biggest challenge to the regime has come from youth-led protests that erupted over the summer – led by a movement that began satirically calling itself the “Cockroaches” – and forced the resignation of one of Modi’s cabinet ministers. Cockroach-led protests hit the streets again last month, also in opposition to the revision of the electoral rolls known as special intensive revision (SIR).

The opposition, in particular the Congress party, has become emboldened in the wake of the youth mobilisation. However, a significant trust deficit remains between the establishment opposition parties and the Cockroach movement. On Sunday, while Cockroach leaders had extended their support to the opposition rally, they did not attend themselves.

Congress leaders had publicly predicted that a million people would attend the rally, but in the end the numbers were in the tens of thousands and empty seats were visible in the protest arena.

The somewhat lacklustre turnout demonstrated the challenge still faced by Congress and its opposition allies in being seen as a formidable and viable challenger to the Modi government.

The opposition, led by Gandhi, has lost the last three general elections to the BJP as the parties have widely failed to counter the BJP’s muscular Hindu nationalist rhetoric. The Modi government has systematically gone after political opponents, leaving little space for dissent.

The majority of those gathered at the protest site on Sunday were party workers and organisers who had been bussed in, and there was very little evidence of a mobilisation of the young people who have been participating in the Cockroach-led protests. According to the opposition, many of their buses were stopped from entering Delhi by police barricades.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Shalini, a restaurant owner from Goa, said she was there “not for the opposition but because I am against the BJP and how they are trying to remove any avenue of dissent in this country. I’m not particularly for any opposition party.”

She said many questions still remained about the viability and support of that opposition, but she sensed a “groundswell of dissatisfaction with the BJP and the opposition is cashing in on it – good for them”.

The issue of the revision of the electoral rolls first emerged last year, but recently erupted into a national scandal after an investigation by the Indian Express newspaper revealed irregularities and discord within the election commission over its implementation.

Millions of allegedly eligible voters have discovered their names have been left off the electoral rolls, with Muslims and minorities disproportionately targeted. This week, Human Rights Watch said the accusations raised “serious questions about the fairness of future Indian elections”.

Arwind Chaudhery, 48, said he too was not aligned with any party. “I am here because SIR has undermined our democracy and the situation has got so bad, it is beyond repair or rectification,” he said. “So it is natural that people will be driven out to protest and that is a ray of hope.”

The BJP have sought to dismiss the opposition protests as anarchy. The party’s president, JP Nadda, said Sunday’s rally would be the “nail in the coffin” for the opposition alliance. He said the protest was “not ​an act of respect for democracy. ‌It is a calculated conspiracy ​to plunge the nation into anarchy, ​all in the name of democracy.”

#138. UK ministers told not to engage formally with Indian mega-zoo after lobbying

Guardian and Mongabay reveal approaches to officials and MPs on behalf of Vantara zoo ahead of UK-chaired Cites meeting on endangered animal imports

British government ministers were told not to engage formally with Vantara, the controversial mega-zoo run by the Indian billionaire Anant Ambani, after lobbying efforts by the private animal sanctuary in the run-up to a UK-chaired meeting on the trade in endangered species, according to documents.

This week, the UK heads a 32-country committee meeting in Geneva of Cites, the United Nations body that regulates trade in endangered species. The meeting will examine how India has responded to compliance concerns about imports of endangered animals.

Documents obtained by the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay under the UK’s Freedom of Information Act reveal Vantara’s efforts to lobby British officials and ministers before the meeting.

Ambani, 31, is the heir to the oil and gas fortune of one of India’s richest men, Mukesh Ambani. He set up a 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo near his family’s oil-refining facilities in Gujarat, where more than 150,000 animals have reportedly been amassed since 2022. Vantara boasts high welfare standards for animals in its care, including facilities such as an elephant hydrotherapy pool, where more than 260 elephants can also receive oil therapies and Ayurvedic massage.

But conservation groups have repeatedly questioned how such a vast number of species, many of which are endangered, were gathered so quickly. Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo’s operators of any wrongdoing.

A subsequent Cites fact-finding mission looking into the zoo raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Its November 2025 report said: “There is a need for caution to ensure that imports of large numbers of animals by these facilities are not inadvertently creating a demand for illegally sourced animals.”

In May, officials from the UK’s Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra) met a representative from Vantara and the US lobbyist firm Checkmate Government Relations, founded by Ches McDowell, a hunting friend of Donald Trump Jr.

Before the meeting, internal briefing documents said Checkmate was “seeking to persuade the UK to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara and of Checkmate’s argument that responsibility for verifying legality sits primarily with exporting or re-exporting parties rather than with India as importer”.

During the meeting, the Vantara representative invited UK officials to visit the zoo – an invitation that was subsequently declined to ensure the impartiality of the UK’s Cites chair role.

After the meeting, a representative from another lobbying firm, Atlas Strategy, working on behalf of Vantara, approached the then secretary of state, Emma Reynolds, and then nature minister, Mary Creagh, also raising the prospect of a visit to the zoo.

When a department official asked for advice, a note was issued recommending that ministers did not engage formally with Vantara.

The government documents also reveal concerns about an application by Vantara to import falcons and falcon hybrids, after an unnamed organisation made a £50,000 “donation” to a breeder in the UK, whose identity was also not disclosed. British Cites officials classified the transaction as commercial, according to the documents. The falcons were not exported to India.

A spokesperson for Vantara said it had not imported falcons from the UK, nor had the zoo hired or paid a lobbying firm. It is understood that Checkmate and Altas Strategy were working pro bono for Vantara and not required to register with the Office of the Registrar of Consultant Lobbyists.

After recent scrutiny, Vantara has sought to “reset” its activities by voluntarily suspending animal imports for a year and hiring John Scanlon, a former Cites chief, to oversee its operations. In an interview with the Sunday Times this year, Ambani said he only brought animals to Vantara legally to ensure their welfare, mentioning a group of hippos he had saved from a hunting lodge in South Africa and jackass penguins saved from a shopping centre in Qatar.

“I think since I was a baby I have been taking care of animals. It’s always been a passion,” he said.

Vantara was used to host Ambani’s lavish pre-wedding celebrations in 2024, which were attended by celebrity guests and politicians. In September, Newcastle University awarded the oil heir a professorship of practice for “his work in conservation, biodiversity protection, animal welfare and environmental stewardship”, sparking a backlash from conservationists.

A Defra spokesperson said: “The UK has strict rules in place to protect endangered species and ensuring international wildlife trade is legal and sustainable, with every application assessed individually against internationally agreed and domestic requirements.”

#135. The private creative world of Rabindranath Tagore, the man Yeats called ‘greater than any of us’

A previously unseen family archive offers a glimpse into the Indian Nobel prize winner’s artistic process and personal relationships in his final years

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

It’s easy to run out of words to describe Rabindranath Tagore. The first non-European to win the Nobel prize in literature, Tagore was a Bengali poet, musician, novelist, philosopher, songwriter, painter, humanist and educator.

Born in 1861, Tagore left an indelible mark on the subcontinent’s literature, music, education and art. Among his enduring legacies are the national anthems of India and Bangladesh.

As Nobel laureate economist Amartya Sen once wrote, Tagore was “a towering figure in the millennium-old literature of Bengal”, whose songs resonate across eastern India and Bangladesh, while his ideas on education, nationalism and freedom of the mind remain relevant today.

Yet his work also made a deep impression on the literary world far beyond the shores of India. WB Yeats is said to have described Tagore as “greater than any of us”. Gitanjali, the collection of poems that won Tagore the Nobel prize that same year, was praised by the committee for its “profoundly sensitive, fresh and beautiful verse”. Tagore’s English translation was published in London that March and reprinted 10 times by the time the prize was announced in November.

Tagore’s life and works have, of course, been well documented, but a collection of material preserved in a private family archive is now being brought into public view for the first time at an exhibition by Christie’s, opening in Mumbai this month. The archive draws from Tagore’s visits to the family home of his protege, Maitreyi Devi, in the bucolic village of Mungpoo in Darjeeling between 1938 and 1940. He died in 1941.

The collection on display – artworks, manuscripts, letters, photographs and personal effects – offers an intimate glimpse into Tagore’s creative process and personal world in his final years.

I spoke to the exhibition curator, Nishad Avari, head of south Asian modern and contemporary art at Christie’s, about the stories behind the archive.

One instructive document Avari points to is a manuscript from 1940. It appears to be a draft of Tagore’s memoir Chelebela or My Boyhood Days, in which he reflects on his life – from growing up in a large family in Calcutta (modern-day Kolkata) to the rapidly changing world ushered in by electricity and the motorcar.

For Avari, what stood out was how the ideas in the document echoed the educational philosophy Tagore put into practice in Santiniketan (or Abode of Peace), the experimental school he set up about 100 miles from the state capital.

“He speaks about how education is like being in prison and that it should be open and free … about the idea of wonder and even fear as inspiring creativity,” Avari told me.

“That’s a direct relationship to Santiniketan and the open model of the school where people sat under trees with their gurus and learned in the open. Education didn’t feel like a restriction, prison or imposition,” he added.

Avari points to another detail that makes this particular document valuable. Of the 10 complete manuscripts in the exhibition, it is the only one dictated to Devi, whose father was a close friend of Tagore’s. For Avari, this speaks to their close relationship and the trust he placed in her.

“If you look at the collection as a whole, she has copies of almost every single one of his publications signed to her by him,” he says. The collection includes hundreds of letters, too: first between Tagore and her father, and later between Tagore and Devi herself.

There are doodles among corrections, Avari explains, which show how his literary practice fed into his artistic practice. Alongside the manuscripts and correspondence are vintage photographs and personal effects, including a kurta and a lungi that Tagore left behind at the family home.

Among the artworks in the collection is a portrait of Devi, particularly notable because Tagore’s figure studies were not generally attributed to specific individuals in the way that portraits by his nephews, Abanindranath and Gaganendranath, often were, Avari said. There is also a landscape depicting the forests of Mungpoo, which Tagore embraced as a retreat.

On the international art market, Tagore’s paintings command significant prices. This year, Sotheby’s sold one of his watercolours for £1.02m (Rs 12.97 crore), nearly four times its low estimate.

Institutions such as Visva-Bharati in Santiniketan and the National Gallery of Modern Art hold much of Tagore’s archival materials, making the discovery of a private archive particularly rare.

He hopes that making these materials public will offer historians, scholars and admirers alike additional perspectives on Tagore. “We hope to add to the understanding of the artist, their career, and in this case, of course, our understanding of the man beyond his art as well.”

Rabindranath Tagore and Mungpoo runs between 10 November and 18 December at Christie’s space in south Mumbai.

Politics | India has invited Pakistan to attend the Shanghai Cooperation Organisation’s Civilisational Dialogue Forum in New Delhi later this month, the Indian Express reported. It is the first invitation since the two neighbouring states engaged in a military conflict last year.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Academia | Indian academic Mahmood Kooria, a lecturer at the University of Edinburgh, is among this year’s recipients of the Philip Leverhulme prize in Humanities. The £100,000 award recognises his work on the premodern Indian Ocean world.

A recommendation from someone with great taste

Freddy Birdy, a copywriter whose delightful words have earned him a devoted Instagram following, recommends writer and historian Malvika Singh’s cookbook, Cooking for My Firefly.

“This book isn’t about recipes, though it does include them. It is a voyage through another time and place, tracing the social fabric of India of the 60s, 70s and 80s, trickling down to today. Told in Malvika’s beautiful prose, it takes us to a time when people entertained in homes, not in restaurants; when dishes were cooked in-house and there were no caterers or party planners. An era long before Instagram posts, WhatsApp invitations, celebrity chefs and food delivery apps, when the point of having a party was simply to have fun. It is an archive of photographs, food, culture, gossip, movie stars, politicians, royalty, set against the spectacular backdrop of Malvika’s dining tables through the decades. I like to dip into it every now and then, much as one might do standing in a dark kitchen with the light of the refrigerator enticing you to midnight goodies.”

Indians lost Rs 22,495 crore (£1.77bn) to cyberfraud in 2025, Times of India reported citing data from the Home Ministry. While the figure is marginally down from the previous year, losses have risen sharply since 2023.

At a time when public anger has spilled into the streets, artist Aamir Aziz’s new track, Dopeher Blues (Afternoon Blues), captures the frustration and angst of a generation that has lived through many crises.

Aziz describes the song as an expression of a period of deep suffocation. Issues such as farmer suicides, unemployment and conflicts in Palestine and Kashmir, he told me, are things he has grown up with even as public attention shifts with the news cycle. It is also a warning, in part, against internet culture, where injustices risk becoming normalised.

Aziz, 35, works in acting, music and writing, and became an internet sensation with his defiant poem Sab Yaad Rakha Jayega (Everything Will Be Remembered) during the citizenship protests. At the heart of his new track is a note of defiance and hope: “We’ll find a way to sing,” he says, as he calls on everyone “to keep speaking up even in a climate of fear, misinformation and oppression.”

#132. Environmentalists criticise choice of Earthshot prize venue owned by fossil fuel firm

Mumbai facility is part of Reliance Industries, owner of world’s biggest oil refinery and funder of controversial mega-zoo

The Prince of Wales’s Earthshot prize will host its forthcoming annual awards ceremony at a venue owned by the billionaire Ambani family conglomerate, drawing criticism from conservationists over their fossil fuel businesses and their foundation’s controversial mega-zoo.

A spokesperson for the Earthshot prize, which awards £1m to five winners every year to invest in conservation work, confirmed to the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay that this year’s ceremony will be held at the Jio World Convention Centre in Mumbai on 17 November.

The venue is part of Mukesh Ambani’s Reliance Industries, which also owns Jamnagar, the world’s biggest oil refinery in Gujarat on India’s western coast. The group’s philanthropic arm funds the 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo Vantara, which was founded by Mukesh’s son Anant Ambani and inaugurated by the Indian prime minister, Narendra Modi, last year. The zoo is embroiled in a long-running controversy over its vast collection of more than 150,000 animals, which reportedly includes some of the world’s most endangered species – from the radiated tortoise, native to Madagascar, to the Spix’s macaw, endemic to north-eastern Brazil but now considered extinct in the wild.

Megan Carr, of the Wildlife Animal Protection Forum South Africa (WAPFSA), warned that using a venue owned by Reliance “could lend an unearned environmental prestige to a corporate group currently under question by the global conservation community”.

Conservation groups, including WAPFSA, have raised concerns about the speed with which Vantara has amassed so many animals, and about how they were sourced. The site, near the family’s oil refinery, houses more than 2,000 species of animals.

This week, the 32-member committee that governs Cites, the UN-administered body that regulates trade in endangered species, requested that India submit another report on the steps it is taking to address concerns relating to its imports of protected species.

The committee also requested the Cites secretariat review 11 cases where there are unresolved questions over how animals were sent to Vantara.

Joanna Swift, the executive director of communications at the Earthshot prize, said it had not sought a philanthropic partnership for its award ceremony venue and that it was paying the standard commercial rate to rent the space.

“The [Jio World Convention Centre] was selected because it allowed us to host the event in Mumbai while meeting the practical requirements of an event of this size. It offers world-class facilities, technical infrastructure and experience of hosting major international events, including environmental ones,” Swift said. “In February this year, JWCC hosted the first ever Mumbai climate week.”

Vantara, Kensington Palace and JWCC have been approached for comment.

Vantara has previously denied allegations of wildlife trafficking and says its purpose is “to rescue wild animals in distress, provide treatment along with lifelong care, and rehabilitate those fit for the wild”.

“With regulatory approval, we also breed select endangered or extinct-in-the-wild species, serving as a modern Noah’s Ark to ensure their survival under human care and restore wild populations through reintroduction,” Vantara says on its website.

Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo of allegations that animals had been acquired illegally or mistreated.

A later Cites fact-finding mission raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Susan Cheyne, the vice-chair of the International Union for Conservation of Nature’s small apes section, said that Vantara’s model has not yet been shown to support conservation and “may be driving demand for wild animals in trade”.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

“For gibbons and other primates, this matters a great deal. Moving animals out of their range countries into a private facility does nothing for wild populations unless there is a transparent, credible plan for those animals: for welfare, for any conservation breeding with clear goals, and for return to range states where that is possible. Real rescue and sanctuary work for primates is done by underfunded centres in range countries,” Cheyne said.

Daniela Freyer, co-founder of the German NGO Pro Wildlife, added: “Our planet is increasingly threatened by the overexploitation of wildlife and by climate change driven by fossil fuels. This week, the Cites Standing Committee, which oversees international wildlife trade, decided to keep India under its compliance procedure, as questions remain about the origin of some of the world’s rarest animals imported to Vantara. A prize whose goals include protecting nature and fixing our climate should be careful not to lend its reputation to these questions.”

Vantara had sought to lobby the UK, the chair of this week’s Cites meetings, attempting to persuade officials to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara, the Guardian and Mongabay reported earlier this month. Ministers were advised not to engage formally with Vantara, internal documents revealed.

The Earthshot prize, founded by Prince William in 2020, is billed as the most prestigious environmental award. William appeared on the BBC’s Graham Norton show last month to promote the prize, saying it was designed to “talk about urgency and optimism together”.

Swift said that it was vital to host an edition of the Earthshot prize in India.

“Mumbai is the sixth largest city on earth. It is home to exceptional environmental solutions and the people behind them, with an impact felt in India and around the world. Spotlighting progress like this, to the largest possible audience, is why the Earthshot prize exists,” Swift said.

This article was amended on 9 November 2026. An earlier version said the zoo Vantara was 400 hectares large; in fact, it is 1,400 hectares.

#131. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.
