#128. Strait of Hormuz could reopen as part of Qatari plan for Iran nuclear inspections

Trump still considering new assault on Iran nuclear sites – though threats less credible after Vance remarks

Qatar, the lead mediator in the US-Iran conflict, is seeking to persuade Tehran to allow UN nuclear weapons inspectors to visit Iran’s bombed nuclear sites, including those housing the country’s stockpile of highly enriched uranium.

Iran would not be required to make any commitment that the stockpile would be removed from the country, with other options on the table, including reducing the uranium content by down-blending it.

Sheikh Tamim bin Hamad al-Thani, the emir of Qatar, spoke to Donald Trump about the proposal, the emir’s office said on Friday without revealing the call’s contents.

The state news agency said the emir “emphasised the importance of continuing dialogue and embracing diplomacy as a means of resolving disputes, preventing further escalation in the region”.

Trump is still considering whether to mount a further assault on Iranian nuclear sites, though the credibility of his threats is weaker inside Iran after his vice-president, JD Vance, said the war itself, and not just its cost to American consumers, was critical to the collapse of the Republican vote in the US midterms.

Under the Qatar proposal, the US would immediately lift the naval blockade on Iran in the strait of Hormuz and release a portion of the $8bn (£6bn) of its frozen assets. In return, Iran would agree to visits by International Atomic Energy Agency inspectors to its bombed nuclear facilities, based on a previous abandoned agreement that had been reached in Cairo in September 2025.

Iran would open the strait of Hormuz on the second day, via a route it has already negotiated with Oman that they would jointly lodge with the International Maritime Organisation (IMO).

Iran has multiple other preconditions for opening the strait including ending the war in Lebanon, a withdrawal of US forces from the Iranian coastline, a commitment to lift some economic sanctions, and agreement to pay war reparations. The sequencing of these commitments has been at the heart of the mediation.

In the memorandum of understanding, a $300bn reconstruction and development fund was to be set up to help Iran recover economically, but it was never clear exactly how this proposal was to be funded.

Iran, in a triumphalist mood after Trump’s election wipeout, has been wary of allowing the UN nuclear inspectors back into the country, claiming it had lost faith in Rafael Grossi, the head of the inspectorate.

Tehran claims Grossi betrayed his duty to uphold the nuclear non-proliferation treaty, by failing to criticise the US for bombing Iran’s nuclear sites. It fears that material gathered by the inspectors would be handed over as targeting data to the Americans and Israelis.

In the parallel talks on opening the strait, Oman’s foreign minister, Badr Albusaidi, spoke again to Iran about the future of the strait administration including the agreed route, the extent to which the agreement is temporary and the need to register the proposal with the IMO, either as a bilateral agreement or a joint regional proposal.

Oman has a long-term proposal for the administration of the strait compliant with the law of the sea in that shipping companies would be charged only for services, and not tolls.

Amir Hayat Moqaddam, the deputy chair of the Iranian parliament’s national security and foreign policy committee, told the Iranian Students’ News Agency (ISNA) on Tuesday that ships from four countries – China, India, Qatar and Iraq – were allowed to pass through the strategic waterway.

The exemptions served mutual interests, he said, adding that Iran charged Indian and Chinese vessels transit fees while allowing Qatari ships to pass free of charge. Medicine was received from India, while China provided political support.

It is reported that of the 10.23m barrels a day of Gulf crude that passed through the strait in October, about 42% went to China and 13% to India, said Muyu Xu, a senior crude oil analyst at the analytics firm Kpler.

The navy of the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) said two “violating” oil tankers attempting to pass through an unauthorised route in the eastern sector of the strait of Hormuz had collided with mines and caught fire.

The IRGC said the two tankers had turned off their navigation and identification systems and that the attempted crossing was with the support of US military forces.

#126. Court rules against Home Office in asylum seeker kidnapping case

Department had claimed that being kidnapped for ransom was not exploitation of trafficking victims in case of Syrian man abducted in Libya

The court of appeal has rejected claims by the Home Office that being kidnapped for ransom is not exploitation of trafficking victims.

The ruling means that many people who have been kidnapped for ransom, whom officials have previously excluded from being victims of exploitation under trafficking rules, will now have to have these claims considered. They may be eligible for the support and recovery package trafficking victims are entitled to.

Being kidnapped for ransom is a common hazard for asylum seekers passing through Libya on their journey to Europe. Many are fleeing conflict in countries such as Sudan and Eritrea as well as some Middle Eastern countries.

The legal challenge was brought by a Syrian asylum seeker who cannot be identified. The 25-year-old fled forced military conscription in July 2021, knowing he was likely to be imprisoned and tortured in Syria for refusing to fight.

He arrived in Libya, ultimately hoping to reach the UK. He and other Syrians were abducted by armed men and held in captivity for a month between August and September 2021. His money and phone were seized and they were driven through the desert for 24 hours, hearing gunshots, with no food and little water.

They were told they would not be released until their families agreed to pay a ransom of several thousand dollars. He was tied up and often beaten on his hands and feet by his kidnappers. He and the other captives had to phone their families to beg them to pay their kidnappers the ransom money. While they were on the phone, the kidnappers beat them so their families could hear screams. He was terrified that even if his family paid his captors, he might not be released and that his organs might be harvested.

The man was released after his family paid his kidnappers, and he made his way to Europe, crossing the Mediterranean from Libya to Italy by boat. He crossed the Channel from France to the UK on 16 May 2022 and claimed asylum.

The Home Office rejected his trafficking claim, arguing that because he had not been subjected to forced labour by his kidnappers, his experience did not meet the definition of exploitation in the trafficking rules. He challenged the decision in the high court, which ruled in his favour. The home secretary, Shabana Mahmood, appealed against the ruling.

While the government has pledged to support trafficking victims, the decision to exclude asylum seekers kidnapped for ransom in Libya has denied many the support the judges ruled they may be entitled to. Recent freedom of information data relating to asylum seekers who were confirmed to be genuine trafficking victims found that of the 1,778 confirmed victims between July 2024 and July 2025, 89% were forcibly removed from the UK, putting them at risk of being trafficked again.

While each trafficking claim must be considered on the facts of the individual case, the judgment found: “The secretary of state erred in not considering the question of whether kidnapping amounted to exploitation.”

The ruling could help thousands of potential trafficking victims, not only those who arrive in Britain in the future but also those previously rejected as being trafficking victims who were kidnapped and held to ransom in Libya. They can now get a fresh referral into the national referral mechanism for a new decision on whether there are reasonable grounds for them to be considered a victim of trafficking.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Jeremy Bloom, a consultant solicitor at Duncan Lewis, who represented the Syrian man, welcomed the ruling. “This judgment is a vindication of my client’s claim to be a victim of trafficking. The Home Office sought to restrict the definition of exploitation,” he said.

“We hope that it will also assist other victims of trafficking who have experienced similarly horrendous treatment to gain access to the leave and support that they need to recover from those experiences. Those individuals may include people who have had their claims to be a victim of trafficking refused, and who might now be able to ask for a new decision based on the law as set out in this judgment.”

Home Office sources said it would not be appropriate to comment on legal proceedings. A Home Office spokesperson said:“We are simplifying and strengthening protection and immigration routes, ensuring they are clear, targeted and focused on their intended purpose, while maintaining fairness and public confidence.”

#125. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#122. Israel’s president picks Yashar leader Gadi Eisenkot to form government

Knesset has been deadlocked since the election last month when Benjamin Netanyahu’s coalition lost its majority

Israel’s president has ⁠chosen Gadi Eisenkot, the prime minister Benjamin Netanyahu’s ⁠strongest rival, to ⁠try to ​form a new government, after a deadlocked election that left neither man ⁠with a clear path to power.

Isaac Herzog’s office said he had informed Eisenkot, the leader of the Yashar party, on Saturday night “of his intention to task him with forming a government”.

It added that the formal appointment would take place in an official ceremony on Sunday at the president’s residence in Jerusalem.

“Now is the time to form a national emergency government. I will do everything to ensure we are worthy,” Eisenkot wrote on X in his first reaction to the announcement of his designation.

Netanyahu ‌is fighting to hold on to office ‌after his religious-nationalist coalition lost its majority in an election on 27 October but still emerged as the largest bloc ‌in parliament.

Should Eisenkot succeed in forming a coalition, he would take over as prime minister from Netanyahu, Israel’s longest-serving premier.

Eisenkot’s wide-ranging opposition bloc won 48 of the Israeli parliament’s 120 seats in October elections, while Netanyahu’s camp secured 53, leaving neither with a majority.

During party consultations with Herzog, 66-year-old Eisenkot received the endorsements of both Arab-majority lists, who could now play kingmakers after making unprecedented gains in the vote.

Eisenkot faces significant challenges on the road to becoming prime minister. He has three options: a power-sharing agreement with Netanyahu, support from Arab-led parties – something several members of his bloc do not support – or an agreement with the ultra-Orthodox parties, which demand military exemptions for their community.

Eisenkot has laid out a series of conditions for any Arab party to join his coalition, including recognition of Israel as a Jewish state and declaring the Palestinian militant group Hamas a terrorist organisation. One of Eisenkot’s partners, the more conservative Yisrael Beiteinu party, already has said it will not join a coalition with Arab-led parties.

Eisenkot, a former army chief, and his partners made equal military service a central part of their campaign. Specifically, they are seeking an end to the longstanding draft exemptions enjoyed by the ultra-Orthodox.

Netanyahu has repeatedly claimed the election was “stolen” by Arab parties, which rallied their supporters to vote in large numbers. But he has offered little to no evidence to support the claims.

Netanyahu is expected to continue his pressure tactics against Eisenkot, saying any alliance with the Arab parties would be an act of betrayal.

Israel’s Shin Bet security agency has launched an investigation into possible foreign interference in the vote, but the country’s Central Elections Committee – and Herzog – say the vote was valid and even if wrongdoing was found, it did not have a significant impact on the result.

Netanyahu called for a “national unity” government with Eisenkot on Saturday night but it remains unclear whether the two rivals could agree on a power-sharing agreement; these types of coalition governments are notoriously fragile and often collapse.

Eisenkot, the military chief of staff from 2015 to 2019, has set out to unite Israelis after Netanyahu’s divisive tenure, under which the country moved sharply to the right.

He has vowed to crack down on extremist West Bank settlers who have attacked Palestinians with impunity, decrying recent incidents as “Jewish terror” abetted by Netanyahu’s government.

But he opposes the creation of a Palestinian state and vowed to end Hamas’s rule in Gaza and destroy the Islamist movement’s military wing.

Commanding the military during the 2006 war with Hezbollah, Eisenkot devised the so-called “Dahiya doctrine”. The idea, named after a Beirut suburb heavily damaged in that war, involves Israel vowing to use massive force against civilian infrastructure in areas controlled by the Lebanese militant movement Hezbollah.

After his appointment, Eisenkot will have 28 days to come up with the 61 votes needed for a government. If he comes up short, Herzog can provide a 14-day extension before he has to offer the mandate to someone else.

If neither side succeeds, Israel will head to fresh elections. The country held five elections between 2019 and 2022 owing to repeated deadlocks.

#120. Germans wrestle with what to do with Hitler's destroyed Eagle's Nest retreat

Fire engulfed Hitler's mountaintop Eagle's Nest retreat in Bavaria last month

Perched on a ridge high up in the spectacular scenery of the German Alps lie the burned-out ruins of a mountain house called The Eagle's Nest, which is now at the heart of an intense debate about Germany's Nazi past.

Known in German as Kehlsteinhaus, the retreat was built by the Nazis in 1938, and frequented by Adolf Hitler and other high-ranking Nazis and their guests. After World War Two it was classified as a historical monument recalling the Nazi era.

Last month it was gutted by fire in a suspected arson attack, and some believe it should be completely destroyed, the site returned to nature and all memory of its Nazi past forgotten.

But others want it either partially or completely rebuilt, wary of the risk of turning it into a site for Neo-Nazi pilgrimage at a time when Germany is witnessing the resurgence of the far-right.

Germany has an "everlasting responsibility to communicate, to deal with our Nazi past", said Isabel Heinemann, professor of contemporary history at Munich's Ludwig-Maximilians-UniversitÃ¤t (LMU).

But rebuilding the site completely, she warned, would mean putting "highly ideologised or highly ideological architecture" back in place.

Adolf Hitler used the mountain retreat to welcome guests

Before it was burned down during the evening of 23 October, the Eagle's Nest had been a popular tourist attraction, owned by the state of Bavaria and operating for many years as a restaurant.

Visitors would take a bus and then ride the original brass and mirror-panelled lift through the mountain to the Kehlsteinhaus to savour the magnificent views above the town of Berchtesgaden.

Once there, they could eat sausages in the beer garden on the Obersalzberg at 1,834m (6,017ft) above sea level, overlooking the Alps.

Unlike Hitler's nearby summer residence, the Berghof, which was bombed in World War Two and demolished in 1952, the Eagle's Nest survived the war.

Tens of thousands of tourists visited each season, providing an important source of income to the region.

In the 1990s, amid concerns that the site did not do enough to confront the crimes of the Nazi past, a museum, the Obersalzberg Documentation Centre, was opened close by.

Before it was burned down, the Kehlsteinhaus or Eagle's Nest had long been a tourist destination in Bavaria

Around 15 years ago, a photo exhibit was opened in the Kehlsteinhaus about its history.

Although the Eagle's Nest itself was "largely destroyed" by the fire, according to police, the documentation centre survived unscathed.

The authorities have not commented on a possible motive, but a 33-year-old man with a history of mental illness has been detained in a secure psychiatric hospital on suspicion of arson.

Man arrested after fire at Hitler's mountaintop Eagle's Nest retreat

Some feel this is the moment to allow the site to fade into history.

"It's been torched now. It's been destroyed. I think they should just clear away the rubble and leave the mountain as it should be in its natural state," said Prof Sir Richard J. Evans, a specialist in Nazi Germany at the University of Cambridge.

That has happened with "quite a few other Nazi sites", he argued, including the bunker in Berlin where Hitler killed himself: "The bunker was filled in with concrete and a children's playground put on the top of it on the ground."

The Kehlsteinhaus was just "a Nazi vanity project" since romanticised by the name Eagle's Nest, he said.

Police say the old Nazi retreat was largely destroyed by last month's fire

But Bavaria's authorities take a different view about the site's future.

Both the state's interior minister, Joachim Herrmann, and its minister for the arts, Markus Blume, have spoken out in favour of rebuilding the Kehlsteinhaus.

"We can hardly leave a ruin standing there," Herrmann said.

Sven Keller, head of the Obersalzberg Documentation Centre, believes it is important not to leave the site in ruins, as that could encourage Neo-Nazi pilgrims, who would be able to hike up there completely undisturbed.

"We have quite some experience at Obersalzberg with what happens if we clear those sites. Hitler's Berghof was demolished in the 1950s, and in our experience, this does not remove such sites from the mental maps of people," he told the BBC's Today programme.

Nor does he believe "a one-on-one reconstruction" of the site is possible, and Blume has made clear on Bavarian radio that "it should not and must not cost the taxpayer anything".

After Hitler's downfall, US soldiers captured the mountain-top retreat and are seen relaxing here on 4 May 1945

Herrmann said they would look for a sensible solution in consultation with historians, given the historically complex circumstances surrounding the building.

Heinemann, who also heads the Leibniz Institute for Contemporary History, believes full-scale reconstruction should be "out of the question".

"I rather think we can document the state left by the fire and also document the historical remains of the Kehlsteinhaus, and then reconstruct parts in a modern design, to make clear the different layers of time that can be seen in this building," she told the BBC.

Germany and Austria have long struggled to deal with the fraught legacy of buildings and sites connected to the Nazi era.

With the recent rise of the far-right AfD and the Austrian Freedom Party, the debate over Erinnerungskultur (Culture of Remembrance) has taken on a new urgency.

A row broke out recently in Germany when AfD and conservative CDU councillors in a town in Saxony decided to ban "stumbling stones" memorials - embedded small brass plaques in the streets - to victims of the Nazis.

In Austria over the summer, a police station was opened in the house where Hitler was born in Braunau am Inn. It followed years of controversy about how the house should be used.

The Austrian government said the police presence would help prevent neo-Nazi pilgrims.

But others had wanted the house to become a centre of responsibility, actively confronting the Nazi past.

As one resident of Braunau am Inn told me, it was a delicate balance between how much to remember and how much to forget.

#119. Gadi Eisenkot reaches out to Netanyahu allies as he strives to form government

Former military chief says prime minister brought Israel to historic crisis and it is approaching ‘point of no return’

Israel’s former military chief Gadi Eisenkot started the tough task of forming a government on Sunday, reaching out to Benjamin Netanyahu’s allies as he said the veteran prime minister had brought the country to a historic crisis.

Eisenkot, 66, the leader of the Yashar party, who has vowed to bridge Israel’s deep polarisation under Netanyahu, formally received a mandate from the president, Isaac Herzog, who gave him 28 days to form a government despite his bloc lacking a clear majority.

“We are in a state of emergency, approaching a point of no return for the state of Israel,” said Eisenkot, who had traded his usual all black for a suit and tie at a ceremony at the presidential office. “We must form a national emergency government. This will require creative and unconventional political steps.”

Eisenkot faces significant challenges on the road to becoming prime minister. He has three options: a power-sharing agreement with Netanyahu, support from Arab-led parties – something several members of his bloc do not support – or an agreement with the ultra-Orthodox parties, which demand military exemptions for their community.

Eisenkot and Herzog acknowledged Israel’s growing isolation under Netanyahu, who is wanted by the international criminal court.

In the US, Israel’s closest ally, Democrats on Tuesday swept the midterm elections, with calls to rein in Israel after the devastation in Gaza and the unpopular Iran war, which Netanyahu launched with Donald Trump.

Eisenkot said: “We are in the midst of a devastating diplomatic crisis. Even our closest friends and strategic allies are saying their relationship with Israel must be reassessed.”

He appealed to Netanyahu’s Likud party as well as the far-right party of the finance minister, Bezalel Smotrich, to join him – but only with himself as prime minister.

“My door is open and my hand is extended, wholeheartedly and willingly, to anyone who understands the gravity of this moment,” he said.

Should Eisenkot succeed in forming a coalition, he would take over as prime minister from Netanyahu, Israel’s longest-serving premier.

Eisenkot’s wide-ranging opposition bloc won 48 of the Israeli parliament’s 120 seats in the October elections, while Netanyahu’s camp secured 53, leaving neither with a majority.

During party consultations with Herzog, Eisenkot received the endorsements of both Arab-majority lists, who could now play kingmakers after making unprecedented gains in the vote.

Eisenkot has laid out a series of conditions for any Arab party to join his coalition, including recognition of Israel as a Jewish state and declaring the Palestinian militant group Hamas a terrorist organisation. One of his partners, the more conservative Yisrael Beiteinu party, has already said it will not join a coalition with Arab-led parties.

Eisenkot, a former army chief, and his partners made equal military service a central part of their campaign. Specifically, they are seeking an end to the longstanding draft exemptions enjoyed by the ultra-Orthodox.

Netanyahu has repeatedly claimed the election was “stolen” by Arab parties, which rallied their supporters to vote in large numbers. But he has offered little to no evidence to support the claims.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Netanyahu is expected to continue his pressure tactics against Eisenkot, saying any alliance with the Arab parties would be an act of betrayal.

Israel’s Shin Bet security agency has launched an investigation into possible foreign interference in the vote, but the country’s Central Elections Committee – and Herzog – say the vote was valid and even if wrongdoing was found, it did not have a significant impact on the result.

Netanyahu called for a “national unity” government with Eisenkot on Saturday night but it remains unclear whether the two rivals could agree on a power-sharing agreement. These types of coalition governments are notoriously fragile and often collapse.

Eisenkot, the military chief of staff from 2015 to 2019, has set out to unite Israelis after Netanyahu’s divisive tenure, under which the country moved sharply to the right.

He has vowed to crack down on extremist West Bank settlers who have attacked Palestinians with impunity, decrying recent incidents as “Jewish terror” abetted by Netanyahu’s government.

He opposes the creation of a Palestinian state and has vowed to end Hamas’s rule in Gaza and destroy the Islamist movement’s military wing.

Commanding the military during the 2006 war with Hezbollah, Eisenkot devised the so-called “Dahiya doctrine”. The idea, named after a Beirut suburb heavily damaged in that war, involves Israel vowing to use massive force against civilian infrastructure in areas controlled by the Lebanese militant movement Hezbollah.

After his appointment, Eisenkot will have 28 days to come up with the 61 votes needed for a government. If he comes up short, Herzog can provide a 14-day extension before he has to offer the mandate to someone else.

If neither side succeeds, Israel will head to fresh elections. The country held five elections between 2019 and 2022 owing to repeated deadlocks.

#118. New trilateral talks on Ukraine war in Abu Dhabi: What to expect?

Ukrainian and Russian representatives last met in February for talks on ending the war, but the same sticking points remain. Ukraine continues to demand security guarantees, which is a non-starter for Russia.

A new round of trilateral talks between Russia, Ukraineand the United States are scheduled to take place in Abu Dhabi on Tuesday and Wednesday, months after a flurry of US-mediated peace talks earlier this year in January and February went nowhere.

When announcing the new trilateral talks last week, US special envoy Steve Witkoff said the discussions would focus on "post-war security arrangements, de-escalation, humanitarian efforts, and next steps toward peace."

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy has named security guarantees, an energy ceasefire, unblocking the Black Sea, extended prisoner exchanges, and preparations for a meeting of the leaders of Ukraine, the United States and Russia as his key topics.

Lawmaker Davyd Arakhamia, a member of Ukraine's delegation, told DW he was not expecting quick progress.

"I don't think there will be a breakthrough, after there was such a long break in the negotiation process," he said. "Simply the fact that trilateral talks are being resumed can be counted as a success."

Witkoff will be joined by US President Donald Trump's son-in-law, real estate developer Jared Kushner, who serves as special envoy.

The Kremlin has sent Vladimir Medinsky, an aide to Russian President Vladimir Putin, to act as chief negotiator, along with a delegation including high-ranking Russian military and intelligence officials.

The Ukrainian delegation will be led by Rustem Umerov, head of Ukraine's Foreign Intelligence Service, along with Arakhamia, and diplomatic and military officials.

Separate formats are planned with several European representatives.

These include Günter Sautter, German Chancellor Friedrich Merz's foreign and security policy adviser, Emmanuel Bonne, head of the Elysee Palace's diplomatic service and Jonathan Powell, the UK's national security advisor, and Fabrizio Saggio, diplomatic adviser to Italian Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni.

Björn Seibert, Chief of Staff for the President of the European Commission, Ursula von der Leyen, and Geoffrey van Leeuwen, Chief of Staff for NATO Secretary-General Mark Rutte, will also attend.

It's doubtful, however, whether European representatives will be formally involved in talks on a potential peace deal.

Hanna Shelest, a security studies director at the foreign policy network Ukrainian Prism, told DW she believed they'd play "an observing role, at best."

She added that, until now, Russia had been reluctant to have European representatives at the table to uphold the impression that talks were "a matter between two superpowers" — namely, Russia and the United States — and to increase pressure on Washington.

"I doubt the Russian delegation will let the Europeans have a say," she told DW.

"Experience from the past years has taught us that as soon as Medinsky heads negotiations, the point becomes demonstrating to the US that Russia is willing to talk, but in reality, they won't reach an agreement," she explained. "Instead, there will be a long sermon about history and all sorts of other things."

However, she said Moscow sending high-ranking intelligence officers as part of the delegation was a "fairly positive aspect" of Russia's intentions.

She believed that additional negotiations were possible at this level.

This could include over prisoner exchanges or humanitarian pauses in villages near Oleshky, a town in the Russian occupied Kherson region in Ukraine's south, where US First Lady Melania Trump was credited with arranging a temporary truce in late October that secured humanitarian aid and allowed civilians to evacuate.

But despite these "positive signals," Shelest cautioned against hoping for concrete results, and suspected Russia was using the talks to stall.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Late last week, Trump spoke of a "big beautiful agreement," after speaking with Russian envoy Kirill Dmitriev, "that might involve all of Europe."

This new security architecture, Kushner later clarified on social media, would be centered around "permanent security consultations, reliable crisis communications, greater military transparency, reciprocal measures to reduce escalation risks, and an enduring framework for long-term European security and stability."

These points "don't contain anything the Russians haven't been talking about since 2009," Shelest said.

Ivan Us, chief consultant and economic diplomacy expert at Ukraine's National Institute for Strategic Studies, pointed out that negotiations with people who are not decisions-makers could be simply for show.

But he agreed with other expert observers that negotiators in Abu Dhabi might try to use the temporary truce brokered for Oleshky as a model for other limited humanitarian ceasefires in the region.

But while Us said he saw cause for optimism, he also cautioned that Russia had continued attacks in Ukraine during the Oleshky truce, and had used the pause to fortify its positions around the town.

#117. Witnesses recount struggle to save people buried in ‘horrifying’ Gaza landslide

High winds and heavy train triggered collapse of sand bank overlooking camp for displaced families

Witnesses have described the frantic struggle to save three members of a family buried by a landslide triggered by high winds and heavy rain in Gaza.

A sand bank overlooking a camp for displaced families south of Deir al-Balah collapsed on Sunday, killing five people and crushing at least three tents.

“It was a horrifying sight,” said Abdul Karim al-Sahhar, 30, originally from Izbat Beit Hanoun in northern Gaza. “We saw people buried beneath the sand and rocks. None of them was moving or making a sound. Even the tent had disappeared completely. There was no trace of it left.”

Among the casualties were an eight-year-old, a six-year-old and their mother.

Sahhar, whose own shelter was badly damaged in the landslide, said the children’s father was outside the family tent and so survived. “One of his children had called him to come and eat with them,” he said. “He was a little late returning, and then the collapse happened.

“The father started shouting: ‘Save my children! Save my children!’ People came and started digging [but] there was a huge boulder on top of the victims. The young men had to break it into pieces so they could reach the people buried underneath.”

Abu Darbali, another witness, said the collapse of the embankment sounded like an explosion. “We found that three tents were covered completely after the mountain collapsed on top of them,” he told Reuters. “People were under the rubble and we couldn’t see them.”

Video posted to social media showed people using hammers to break large rocks and others removing sand with shovels, using flashlights to search for those trapped underneath.

The UN said more than 26,000 households had reported damage to shelters or belongings after the weekend’s rainstorms.

The vast majority of Gaza’s population of 2.3 million live in tent encampments or the ruins of bombed-out buildings, ‌with reconstruction yet to begin after Israel’s relentless two-year military assault launched after the Hamas attacks on southern Israel in October 2023.

“The collapse [this weekend] was waiting to happen. [Gaza] is extremely overcrowded. People are just trying to find a place wherever,” said Toby Fricker, a spokesperson for Unicef, which has been working to prevent similar tragedies and improve drainage in camps for displaced people.

In a statement, the Hamas-run government media office said about “70,000 tents sheltering tens of thousands of displaced people were flooded on the first day” of rains. It also noted the “collapse of dozens of cracked walls and roofs, which caused injuries”, some critical, in various areas of the tiny territory.

A US-brokered ceasefire last year called for the eventual reconstruction of Gaza, but little progress has been made. Negotiators have failed to agree on terms for Hamas to disarm or for Israel to withdraw its troops, which control about two-thirds of the territory ‌from which residents have been ordered to leave.

Israel says no reconstruction can happen before Hamas is disarmed.

Sahhar said he and his family were now homeless. “My tent and everything I own are now buried under the sand. My mattress, household utensils and all my belongings – everything was buried. I could not retrieve anything,” he said.

Mustafa Qazzaat, a member of the Gaza City council, said Israel had not allowed prefabricated homes to shelter the displaced since the truce was announced, and only permitted the entry of limited numbers of tents.

Many so-called “dual use” items remain restricted, such as metal tent poles. Humanitarian workers said they instead had used wooden poles fashioned from palettes used to transport food.

Cogat, the Israeli defence ministry body that oversees aid to Gaza, said more than 91,000 tents and over 8,200 trucks containing tarpaulins, blankets and winter clothing had reached Gaza, as well as convoys carrying supplies of fuel and engine oil to power pumping facilities and other humanitarian installations.

Earlier this year, an assessment compiled by shelter specialists in Gaza revealed that thousands of tents supplied by China, Egypt and Saudi Arabia to shelter displaced Palestinians offered only limited protection against rain and wind.

#116. The Trump coalition is shattered - what will Republicans do now?

Republicans are in soul-searching mode after the coalition that powered Donald Trump to three straight presidential nominations imploded in spectacular fashion in the 2026 midterms.

The stunning election defeat immediately raised questions about the party's future post-Trump.

But there are no easy answers as Republicans face the choice of doubling down on Trumpism or trying to chart a new path forward in his final years in office.

The depth of the problem was captured by outgoing Florida Governor Ron DeSantis, who called the midterms "close to an extinction-level event for Republicans".

Others struck a more optimistic tone. Vice-President JD Vance, who blamed the midterm results on public anger over the war with Iran, argued the party could turn things around. Nevertheless Vance acknowledged the fallout will be messy.

"There will be a lot of infighting and arguing over what happened and why," Vance said.

The wide range of reactions on the right highlights deep fault lines in the party that formed around Trump's improbable rise to the presidency in 2016 and never really went away.

It's too early to tell which faction will win. But while Republicans are still licking their wounds, the intraparty battle Vance predicted is already under way.

The party must decide whether it will be "centred on one figure, one guy, or on a wider set of ideas like conservatism that can win outside the deepest red places," Rina Shah, a Republican strategist, told the BBC.

Critics on the right - the "Never Trumpers" who opposed his first White House campaign - are pushing for a return to the Republican Party's pre-Trump roots, citing the historic losses around the country on election night.

But simply turning back the clock on Trump will be extremely difficult if not impossible to do.

The party has been wholly transformed during Trump's decade-long reign. Most Republicans have shifted to the right on domestic issues like immigration, and rejected the interventionist foreign policy of the George W Bush era that led to the wars in Iraq and Afghanistan.

In Congress, the more moderate establishment wing of the party that disliked Trump from the start has almost entirely disappeared, replaced by conservative Trump backers who echo his nativism and populist rhetoric.

That trend continued this year, as old-guard Republicans like John Cornyn in Texas and Maine's Susan Collins, both of whom clashed with Trump over the years, were voted out of office.

Three days after the election Trump appointed Stephen Miller as his new White House chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

The move signalled Trump has no plans to moderate his policies or message in his remaining years in office. Miller, who had been serving as the president's chief domestic policy adviser, is a longtime Trump aide known for his hardline immigration policies.

With Stephen Miller pick, Trump readies for a battle

One of the architects of the Make America Great Again (Maga) movement, Steve Bannon, predicted a "civil war" in the party after the midterms.

Afterwards, he blamed the Republicans' defeat on the economy and "foreign wars". "I don't think we implemented the Maga programme," he said, calling on Trump to double down on mass deportations.

But other Republican strategists said the midterms were a wake up call for Make America Great Again (Maga) loyalists who want Trump's political movement to outlast his presidency.

"There's still plenty of Maga voters out there who have supported Donald Trump through thick and thin," said Whit Ayres, a Republican strategist.

"But there is also a significant portion of the Republican Party that's non-Maga, and that's a majority now."

The party lost control of the House and Senate, as well as governorships and state legislatures in red states like Texas and Kansas where Democrats had been shut out of power for decades - and where Trump won by double digits when he was on the ballot.

The stunning upsets underscored the fickle nature of political coalitions.

Trump expanded the Republican base in 2024, increasing support with Latinos and younger voters among other key groups. Republicans hailed the gains as a sign of future strength for the party.

Just two years later, the party lost ground with nearly every critical voting bloc. According to a New York Times analysis of voter data, Latino support for Democratic candidates increased by 29 percentage points from 2024-26. Support from young voters 18-29 years old increased by 33 points.

The swings reflected polling ahead of the midterms that found widespread disapproval with Trump's handling of the economy, immigration and the war with Iran.

Tuesday night saw Trump's Republicans lose in states they had comfortably held for decades

Democrats also capitalised on a public perception that the Trump family and administration is rife with self-dealing and corruption. The president's focus on the White House ballroom and other Washington construction projects also contributed to a sense among voters that he was more focused on his legacy than addressing affordability issues.

It's a sentiment shared by Benjamin Shrader, the leader of the Young Republicans in Austin, who was still shell-shocked when he spoke to the BBC in Texas the morning after his party was almost wiped out in the state by a Democratic "blue tsunami".

Shrader said Trump was still a "force of nature" when asked to reflect on how the president had used his first public appearance after the results to show off the White House ballroom project.

He hesitated before adding: "I would focus on issues that are not ballrooms, that are not renaming things. I would focus on what matters."

The evidence in the immediate aftermath of the midterms suggests that Republicans could benefit by distancing themselves from Trump ahead of 2028.

With Congress flipped, what does Donald Trump do next?

Vance tried to thread the needle with his midterm assessment blaming the Iran war and calling for a return to "America First." It was a nuanced critique that broke with Trump on an unpopular war, but embraced a broader political brand that remains popular with the Maga base.

Trump's next moves on Iran will be watched closely by the party for their 2028 implications. An escalation would risk fuelling the concerns voiced by Vance and other "America First" proponents.

In his postmortem statement on X, Vance also echoed the growing calls on the right to reexamine US support for Israel. But he also signalled an openness to making some immigration enforcement changes. That clashes with Miller, a Vance ally who has speared the administration's second-term immigration agenda.

Party strategists said the midterm drubbing complicated Vance's path to the nomination.

"Before this midterm, JD Vance was the odds-on favourite to win the Republican nomination in 2028. After this midterm, the nomination battle is completely wide open," Ayres said.

Potential Trump successors have some time to consider their options, but they'll have to make up their minds relatively soon. Any 2028 presidential hopefuls will face pressure to announce White House bids in the next few months. Some could launch campaigns before the end of the year.

Vance's election analysis was widely interpreted as an unofficial signal of his plans to run. It will likely be a crowded primary field.

Both Vice-President JD Vance and Secretary of State Marco Rubio are seen as contenders for the 2028 nomination

Other rumoured White House contenders include Secretary of State Marco Rubio, Senator Ted Cruz of Texas, Florida Governor DeSantis, former US ambassador to the United Nations Nikki Haley and former New Jersey Governor Chris Christie, to name a few.

In the next election cycle, Republican presidential and congressional candidates alike will have tough decisions to make on key issues of the day, from US support for Israel to tariffs to the AI boom.

Trump's power may be diminished as he enters the final two years of his second term, but he'll still have the loudest voice in the party over the next two years. It's unclear whether he will get deeply involved in the process or take a hands-off approach.

Trump has praised Vance and Rubio at different times, but typically deflects questions about who he'd like to see succeed him.

His endorsement will loom as the biggest wildcard in the 2028 Republican primary race.

"It remains to be seen who will jump into that race," Ayres said.

"But I think it's going to be a lot larger and more diverse than we anticipated before the midterm election."

'Hopefully things get done': Americans react to historic political shift

#113. Polish prosecutors say ‘too early’ to tell if Kraków gunman was motivated by political ideology

Terrorism investigation will continue after suspect who killed six after opening fire in market square died

Polish prosecutors have said they do not currently have any evidence tying the man who killed six people and injured 16 in a rare mass shooting in Kraków to any particularly political ideology.

The 24-year-old Polish national died in hospital early on Monday after being critically injured in a shootout with police officers as they apprehended him after the attack in the city’s busy market square on Saturday afternoon.

Soon after the attack, the bodies of the gunman’s parents and sister were found at the family home in Ostrowiec Świętokrzyski, a town about 100 miles north-east of Kraków. Police said they had probably died of gunshot wounds.

Poland’s interior minister, Marcin Kierwiński, said on Saturday that the gunman, named in multiple reports as Michał Oksiński, was fascinated with far-right ideologies and held “very radical” and “ultra-nationalistic” views.

Polish prosecutors opened a terrorism investigation, which the justice minister, Waldemar Żurek, said would continue despite the gunman’s death and would aim to establish if he had acted alone or had been inspired by any group.

However, at a press conference on Monday, prosecutors said there was no evidence tying the suspect to any particular political bent.

“We do not have findings that we could, in a responsible way, categorically state that he was inspired by any particular ideology,” said the national prosecutor’s office spokesperson, Przemysław Nowak.

“It is far too early to comment on the motives … We have not found any obvious symbols that are generally associated with a particular political movement, whether far right or far left,” he added.

An interior ministry spokesperson told Polish radio that the “many emblems and patches” on Oksiński’s clothing were being analysed as part of the investigation.

Żurek also called for “restraint” earlier on Monday amid the ongoing investigation. “Evidence is ​being collected, ​but I will not ​show or disclose it. I ask for restraint,” he told Radio ‌Zet.

Wearing military-style clothing and a bulletproof vest, the gunman drove at high speed into the square in the heart of the old town, which is usually packed with tourists, and started shooting at random in a “wild ride” around the square, the Kraków police spokesperson Katarzyna Cisło said.

The shooting has shocked a country that had been considered safer than many of its European neighbours, and has deepened concern after several school stabbings in October that left one woman dead and which officials said were linked to extremist content online.

Among the six victims were a 32-year-old British man and an Austrian woman aged 51. The other four victims were Polish nationals.

Of the 16 injured, 15 were in a stable condition as of late on Sunday, including two police officers who were brought out of medically induced comas earlier in the day. Five of the injured were from Britain, Austria, Germany, Portugal and Russia.

Police said the suspect, who had no criminal record and held a firearms licence, initially continued shooting even after he was wounded by police during the exchange of fire. The two injured police officers – Jakub Kulik, a former paratrooper and volunteer firefighter, and Artur Morański– were hailed as heroes for their rapid response and for preventing further casualties.

Police confirmed on Sunday that three people found dead at the gunman’s home were his father, 53, mother, 52, and sister, 21.

Mourners have left candles in tribute to the victims in the square, which reopened late on Sunday. Flags across the country were lowered to half mast during two days of national mourning, as were European flags outside the European Commission in Brussels “in solidarity with the Polish people”, the commission said.

#112. What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

One of the Artificial Intelligence (AI) industry's most important figures has been accused of having an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, prompting an investigation.

US intelligence officials believed that Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang, who had a close relationship with Nvidia CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report.

On Sunday, Huang admitted, external to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

US tech giant Nvidia, one of the most valuable companies in the world, has opened an independent investigation into the reports. The firm said it does not believe US national security had been compromised - and added that Huang was committed to continuing to lead the company.

President Donald Trump said on Monday that the FBI is also investigating the affair.

The FBI declined to comment further when contacted by the BBC.

Trump said he spoke with Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Here is what we know about the affair and why it matters.

Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Jensen Huang (left) and Alice Huang - also known as Huang Le - pictured at an event

US officials believed that Alice Huang had been recruited to spy by China's top intelligence agency, the New York Times reported, external. Her current whereabouts are unknown.

Alice Huang, who is of no relation to Jensen Huang, was a top executive at Singapore-based data centre firm Megaspeed between March and November 2023. The firm had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China, a concern that Nvidia maintains was investigated and debunked.

However, the BBC understands Megaspeed is under investigation by the Singapore government regarding previous allegations that the company was circumventing US sanctions on the export of Nvidia's high-end chips. The BBC has contacted Megaspeed for comment.

The company told the New York Times last month its current leadership took the allegations seriously and had appointed external legal advisers to conduct an internal investigation.

Surveillance reportedly revealed communications between Alice Huang and an American citizen, who was subsequently identified as Jensen Huang through a process known as "unmasking".

It remains unclear what, if any, intelligence Alice Huang â who is also known as Huang Le â allegedly gathered and passed to Beijing. She did not respond to the paper's request for comment.

When the BBC asked Nvidia if the company was aware of intelligence concerns, the company said it was not.

"We trust that if we were the target of a genuine threat, the US government would have notified us," an Nvidia spokesperson told the BBC.

China has rejected the spying allegations.

Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun told reporters on Monday at a regular daily press briefing: "We have already responded to this. The allegations about Chinese spies are entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China."

The New York Times reported in late October that Trump had been briefed last year on intelligence alleging Alice Huang was spying on China's behalf.

Asked by reporters on Sunday whether he wished he had "acted on the intelligence", Trump responded: "No, I don't think so. I think he's an honourable guy."

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio, meanwhile, refused to comment when asked about Huang. Last spring, the secretary of state reportedly oversaw the drafting of a memo that warned the Trump administration about Huang's relationship with the Chinese businesswoman, according to the New York Times.

Nvidia shares slide as US tech giant investigates CEO's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Why Trump is all-in on AI despite the warnings

What is AI, how does it work and why are some people concerned about it?

Nvidia is at the heart of US and China's AI race, which is why the billionaire's affair has sparked national security fears among American lawmakers.

Trump has said "whoever wins AI wins", and the fear is that if China gains the upper hand in AI, it will supercharge the country's military and surveillance capabilities.

The US advantage is in large part because of US companies like Nvidia, working with producers like the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC), which makes more than 90% of the world's most advanced chips.

Since 2022, the US has used various export controls to keep the top tech out of China, which it says helps retain America's competitive edge and protects its national security.

This means companies like Nvidia have been largely locked out of the world's most lucrative market, with Huang once saying that Nvidia's share of the Chinese market had gone from around 95% to effectively zero.

Huang has engaged in shuttle diplomacy between Washington and Beijing, hoping to find a US-approved way back in, and shaping US policy on China along the way.

Sunday's revelations may explain why Huang has failed to bring home the biggest prize of all to Nvidia: the Trump administration's blessing to sell all but its most advanced chips to China.

Huang has soared to international importance with his company

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang was nine years old when his family moved to the West Coast of the US. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

Huang then worked at US chip firm AMD before co-founding Nvidia. Known as "Daddy Huang" in Taiwan and once lauded as the "Taylor Swift of tech", he has a reputation as a workaholic and perfectionist with incredibly high standards for his staff.

He has an estimated fortune of $155bn (Â£115bn), according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Huang, told the BBC, adding that he was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

Huang has a famously close relationship with Trump. At the recent AI summit at the White House, where 42 tech executives were present, the president was flanked by Elon Musk and Huang.

Trump said he had not spoken to the Nvidia boss since news of the investigation emerged.

"I just know he's a fine person, but you never know what's going on," Trump told reporters, adding that he had "done an amazing job for our country".

There is no clear successor for Huang at Nvidia

Founded by Huang and fellow electrical engineers Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem in 1993, the company was originally known for chips that process graphics, particularly for computer games.

Eventually it changed focus to AI, a field it now dominates.

Huang rescued Nvidia from bankruptcy on more than one occasion, and took risks on AI a long time before the rest of the world did, effectively building the infrastructure from scratch.

"Today when we think of that, we think of these giant data centres - none of that existed. There were no customers for the stuff he was building. In fact his first customer was Open AI at the time they had 30 people - he actually gave it to them, he donated it to them," Witt told the BBC.

Every notable tech company building AI tools and infrastructure, including Amazon, Meta, Google, and Microsoft, now use Nvidia chips.

Currently worth $4.5tn (Â£3.4tn), Nvidia is one of the most valuable companies ever.

For now, the firm's board appears to have little appetite for removing Huang, saying in a statement on Sunday that they have "confidence in Jensen and his leadership team".

Sources tell the BBC his removal is unlikely because there is so much money at stake.

There is no public succession plan for the 63-year-old. He has often said he hopes to continue in the role for decades, and Bloomberg reports he has created an informal leadership structure around him unlike a traditional company's hierarchy.

His children, Spencer and Madison, and son-in-law Nico Caprez work at Nvidia in senior roles.

Lori Huang accompanied her husband at the state dinner held by President Donald Trump for his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping in September

Huang met his future wife Lori Mills at Oregon State University while studying electrical engineering in the early 1980s. The pair were lab partners.

They married in 1985, about eight years before he co-founded Nvidia, and have two children, Madison and Spencer.

In 2007, the couple founded the Jen-Hsun and Lori Huang Foundation. The charitable fund has seen its assets surge in recent years, driven by the soaring value of Nvidia shares, and is estimated to be worth more than $9bn.

Its donations include tens of millions of dollars to engineering programmes at Oregon State and Stanford University.

Witt told the BBC that Huang has gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, adding: "That's very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," he added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

Additional reporting by Tom McArthur in London, Ayeshea Perera and Tessa Wong in Singapore and Helen Davidson in Sydney

Nvidia boss rejects AI extinction fears as 'doomsday narratives'

China is winning one AI race, the US another - but either might pull ahead

#111. Nvidia shareholders sue Jensen Huang over alleged spy ties

Nvidia shareholders have sued the chipmaker and its CEO, Jensen Huang, over his alleged ties to a suspected Chinese spy. The case adds to scrutiny over whether sensitive information at the AI giant was compromised.

Shareholders sued chipmaker Nvidia and its chief executive, Jensen Huang, on Monday, accusing them of concealing his alleged relationship with a suspected Chinese intelligence asset.

The proposed class action adds to investigations into whether Huang was targeted by foreign intelligence, raising questions about the security of sensitive information at the world's most valuable chipmaker.

The complaint, filed in federal court in San Jose, California, alleges that Nvidia and Huang misled investors about his relationship with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based Megaspeed who is not related to him.

Shareholders say the defendants failed to disclose national security concerns that threatened Nvidia's reputation and business prospects. They are seeking unspecified damages.

The New York Times reported last month on the alleged relationship and said US authorities had concluded that Alice Huang was a Chinese intelligence asset.

Nvidia announced an independent investigation Sunday into reports that Huang had been the "target of a foreign intelligence effort."

Huang acknowledged being unfaithful to his wife in an open letter, but did not identify the woman.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"I support the investigation and will cooperate fully," he said. "I am committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security."

Nvidia shares closed down 4.3% Monday. The lawsuit was filed five minutes after US markets closed.

US President Donald Trump said Monday that the FBI was investigating the matter, while expressing support for Huang.

Trump said he had spoken to the executive earlier that day.

"He said he had absolutely no idea that the person in question was a spy," Trump told reporters.

China denied conducting an intelligence operation targeting Huang.

"The so-called Chinese spy allegation is unsubstantiated. It is nothing but smears against China," Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun said.

Huang co-founded Nvidia and led its transformation from a maker of gaming chips into a leading supplier of the computing technology powering artificial intelligence.

Its advanced chips have become a focus of US-China competition, with Washington restricting exports to limit Beijing's access to the technology.

Huang has pushed for looser restrictions on sales to China. The investigations have intensified scrutiny of his leadership and whether confidential company information was compromised.

#109. Labour’s Brexit red lines ready for ‘dignified retirement’, says their co-author

Exclusive: MP Chris Ward believes ditching pledges and forming closer ties with EU would protect it from global threats

One of the architects of Keir Stamer’s Brexit red lines has called for Andy Burnham to abandon them at the next election, saying a new relationship with the EU would protect the UK from global threats.

The call is the latest voice from a prominent figure in Starmer’s administration for a faster transition to a far closer European relationship, after the former chancellor Rachel Reeves said it was right for Burnham to consider rejoining.

Chris Ward, one of Starmer’s closest confidants in politics and one of the ministers who worked on the EU reset, said that it was the security relationship that meant a change was now imperative.

The Labour MP for Brighton Kemptown and Peacehaven said it was now time to give a “a dignified retirement” to the red lines, which rule out rejoining the EU, the single market, the customs union and restoring freedom of movement.

Ward also suggested there was little compelling argument for rejoining the single market without full EU membership, saying he had “never understood the argument for the latter without the former”.

But he said the main reason for abandoning the red lines was that the UK “could no longer rely on the Americans” which was a major geopolitical shift that merited an entire reappraisal of Britain’s diplomatic relationships. “That question was unthinkable in 2016, but it is unavoidable in 2026,” he wrote in an article for the Guardian.

Ward, who was Starmer’s chief adviser as shadow Brexit secretary and who co-authored the red lines in Labour’s manifesto in 2024, said that they had now outlived their usefulness and should no longer be part of Labour’s future thinking.

He remains close to the former prime minister and said that the drive to renew the relationship with the EU may prove the defining political moment of this autumn.

He said the party had agreed upon the red lines in the manifesto because they were “designed precisely to close down the debate and to narrow the target that Brexit had become for Labour”.

“I still believe that strategically this was the right course for Labour, and the reset deal that – touch wood – will get over the line at next month’s UK-EU summit will demonstrate that real benefits can be delivered within those red lines, whether cheaper prices for businesses, a youth mobility scheme or the return of the Erasmus+ scheme.

“By the next general election, however, those red lines will have served their purpose. They should be given a well-earned and dignified retirement. Frankly, they no longer speak to the world as it is in 2026, let alone as it may be over the years to come.”

Ward said it was well known that rejoining the EU or single market would have economic benefits but said supporters of rejoining should make a different argument.

“Pinning the case for a new relationship on old arguments risks both the same result and missing what’s really changed,” he wrote.

“Instead, its the huge geopolitical shifts and, above all, the new security and cyber threats the UK and Europe now face day in, day out that demand we now reassess the depth and nature of relationship.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

He said Starmer had twice had to scramble alliances together – on Ukraine and on the strait of Hormuz – because “we could no longer rely on the Americans”.

“It would now be brave in the extreme to continue to pin our future security – and that of Europe – on the outcome of US elections, or the false hope that whoever is in the White House we can still bank on the US as we did in 2016, let alone 1945.”

Ward said there should be public debate on whether the UK should formally rejoin or seek a form of associate membership like Canada.

But he said the prime minister will “need to be clear as to why now is the right time to reopen the Brexit debate, what we are seeking, and why it is so important to our national interest. A re-run of the tired arguments of 2016 won’t do that. Defending red lines designed for 2020 won’t do that either. But appreciating the urgent strategic challenges of the decades to come just might.”

Burnham will attend the European Political Community summit in Dublin on Thursday, expected to be used to see Labour’s reset deal over the line and to meet many European leaders for the first time.

Burnham will also meet European leaders on the sidelines to set out his ambitions for further integration after he announced his intention to seek a closer and more permanent relationship – including potentially rejoining the bloc.

At a Brexit reset meeting on 20 November in Brussels, the UK will sign new agreements on agriculture and food, emissions trading and a youth mobility scheme.

The prime minister is then expected to give a speech after the summit on options for the future relationship. Burnham has said previously an option to rejoin the single market could be offered in a Labour manifesto but fully rejoining would need a referendum.
