#108. Inside the French drug gangs recruiting children to kill

Watch: "It destroys people, but we all make our own choices" - Marseille drug-gang members talk to BBC

Warning: Strong language and descriptions of sexual violence

Although she had just bought hashish from a drug dealer, the woman decided to call the police.

It was because, along with the illegal drugs, she had been handed a note by the young seller during their brief meeting on a Marseille housing estate in 2022.

"We're being held captive and need help. We are 15-years-old," it read.

The teenage dealer, a French court heard earlier this year, was one of two boys being held by a gang which they had both initially approached looking for paid work - albeit illegal.

One boy had been tasked with handling money, the other worked streets around the Frais-Vallon estate. Soon though, bosses had accused them of being responsible for "shortfalls" in takings.

What followed were regular beatings, the court was told, and being forced to dance naked in front of a camera as a form of blackmail. One of the boys was also raped.

Hours after the woman was passed the note, the two teenagers had tried to escape from a second-storey window, injuring themselves in the process. One managed to get away, while the other was caught, beaten and stabbed, but survived.

Arrests were made. Photos on the phone of one of the boys' alleged torturers showed several other young men in a similarly distressed condition. Six men have since been handed hefty sentences for what happened to the two teenagers.

The boys' story highlights the grip that drug gangs hold over parts of France's second city.

While the blend of Mediterranean culture, street art and hip-hop scene has made Marseille a popular tourist destination - beyond the city centre, it is another story.

After months of trying, a BBC team managed to enter this dangerous world - centred on deprived streets and high-rise housing estates in the port city, which police do not enter lightly.

We found an environment where ruthless gangs lure vulnerable young people with promises of easy money, but end up trapping them in a world of drug dealing, brutality and exploitation.

We saw how gangs use social media to recruit hitmen and spoke to several young men caught up in the violence. From prison, one gang member accused of involvement in several murders explained how his "cold-bloodedness" had helped him rise through the ranks.

A still image from one of DZ Mafia's notorious video statements

The city's most powerful gang, DZ Mafia, emerged victorious from a bloody turf war and is now one of the most feared criminal organisations in France.

DZ is the official internet country code for Algeria, where one of the gang's founders, a French-Algerian called Mehdi Laribi, was detained earlier this year under a French arrest warrant.

The criminal outfit rose to prominence in 2023, when it became the first French gang to capitalise on its notoriety by releasing video statements - using social media to market itself as a brand - says the head of the country's anti-drug trafficking unit, Dimitri Zoulas.

Its most recent statement came in July, when the mayor of a nearby town had to be placed in police protection, after intelligence suggested he was in danger of being targeted by the group.

In an online video, six masked men dressed in black stood behind a table - as one of them read a statement denying DZ Mafia's involvement.

Inside Marseille's drug gangs - watch on YouTube now, external

France's expanding illegal drugs market - which has seen a surge in cocaine imports alongside high volumes of cannabis and synthetic drugs - has also helped the gang grow rapidly.

The number of cocaine users in France is expected soon to reach two million, says Zoulas, up from 600,000 in 2017. Drug smuggling across France generates â¬7bn (Â£5.9bn) a year, involving 200,000 people, he adds.

Many of those distributing the drugs are children and the authorities say the number of minors caught up in trafficking is increasing. During 2025, more than 10,000 children were involved in some way, according to official figures.

DZ Mafia is a prime example. To reach more customers, it needs more gang members - more children.

We have found it is now recruiting young people from across France - not just to sell drugs, but to carry out kidnap, torture and murder.

They are a generation seen as easy to corrupt, but also expendable, by the kingpins who typically control them from prison or overseas.

And, as police and judges warn, once these children enter Marseille's underworld, it is very hard to get them out.

Karine was called by a doctor about her son Hugo: "Your son arrived last night. He took a bullet to the head"

Few know the human cost of Marseille's gangs like mother Karine Brouillard - who lives at the other end of France, almost as far from the Mediterranean coast as you can get.

Her son, Hugo, first ran away aged 15 - from his home in a village in the Nord department - after an argument with his family. He was picked up by police three days later, almost 1,000km (600 miles) away in Marseille, with money and cannabis on him. He was taken home.

But several years later, in 2023, Hugo planned to return south, telling his parents he had secured a holiday job at a restaurant near Marseille.

"He gave regular updates," Karine remembers. "He called me, everything was going well."

Three weeks later, a doctor called. Karine says she will never forget his words: "Your son arrived last night. He took a bullet to the head."

Police believe Hugo was brought for medical help from a gang-controlled area and suspect he was involved with one.

DZ Mafia wields power in Marseille's Quartier Nord

Our best way into Quartier Nord, where DZ Mafia wields power, is with the help of resident Fadella Ouidef, a 41-year-old mother-of-four who gives us a tour.

Thirty-seven seconds after entering one housing estate, we find ourselves cornered.

Car horns sound the moment Fadella's Peugeot passes a gang checkpoint - the teenagers manning the post immediately spot our camera. "Wait, stop filming," Fadella warns, as a grey 4x4 forces us to the side of the road.

"I'm making a report for my housing association," she tells the inquisitive driver. He eventually agrees to let us leave if we stop filming.

It is unnerving to see a non-police vehicle move so assertively and a relief to watch it leave.

"Lately, the gangs have been very aggressive," explains Fadella. "What we simply try to do is make sure our young people understand what's happening is wrong."

Fadella speaks to a wider fear among local families: that their children are enticed by huge sums of money to take a criminal path that robs them of their innocence.

Camil, who claims to have left DZ Mafia, only agreed to speak to the BBC away from the city - on a boat

Money is what led one young man to spend his teenage years with DZ Mafia.

In order for our interviewees to speak freely, or because of safety concerns, we have agreed to protect their identities.

Camil tells us he worked up the gang's ranks to "running security" in one area it controls.

The now 20-year-old, who claims to have left the group, is highly suspicious of our presence, but agrees to tell us how the gang recruits young members.

"They know how to get inside your head. Whatever you want, they give to you," he says. This can range from nice cars and spacious apartments, to drugs and prostitutes, he explains.

"You feel loyalty because of what they give you. You'll stay until the very end, until either death or prison."

Camil also gives a sobering assessment of what happens if the gang identifies a weakness in a recruit: "They'll exploit it. You're their bitch, it's over."

DZ Mafia member, Adam - not his real name - tells us he was part of a DZ Mafia hit squad when we speak to him in a Snapchat video call.

He is currently in prison awaiting trial, after being implicated in several murders.

"People noticed my determination, my cold-bloodedness and, without wanting to, I climbed up the ladder of the gang," says Adam. "It became my place, I had to roll with it."

During the chat, he agrees the killings he has been charged with were "quite shocking".

"Attempts in broad daylight, in traffic jamsâ¦ it can range from going to hit a guy, slap him to taking him in front of his mother and burning him up in a car. I think I have good composure, I handle it fairly well."

"Thank you, see you soon" reads graffiti on a wall, alongside price lists for cocaine, hashish and marijuana

We challenge Adam on whether he feels regret when carrying out such acts of brutality.

He says he is "feeling in-part guilty" but claims to have no regrets.

"If I hadn't done all of this, there are other people who would have done that in my place, and so on. I am just another brick in the wall," he says.

Like so many teenagers from Marseille, he knew large sums of money could be earned quickly in drug trafficking. Now in his twenties, he admits the environment he entered was a "trap".

At a so-called deal point in a social housing estate - sealed off and controlled by DZ Mafia - price lists for cocaine, hashish and marijuana are graffitied on a wall.

The estate acts as a natural fortress for drug dealers. With one major road in and out, they can control the flow of customers and see if the police are coming.

It is not uncommon for the sound of automatic gunfire to fill the night air in these areas.

After months of negotiations, a deal-point manager from a rival gang agrees to meet us where he works.

Gang member "Joe" says he was making â¬3,000 (Â£2,500) per month aged 13

We enter the estate early in the morning, the birdsong and children walking to school providing a stark contrast to the industrial-scale criminal operation that takes place here.

"Joe" says he was making â¬3,000 (Â£2,500) a month when he was 13. Now he says that has risen to between â¬5,000 and â¬10,000 a month.

He seems to see his life path as pre-ordained, albeit a lucrative one.

We ask Joe if he thinks about the lives lost because of the violence carried out by his gang, but the 23-year-old has also long made peace with the pain and destruction drug trafficking can cause.

"It destroys people, but we all make our own choices," he shrugs.

The work that Joe's gang and DZ Mafia are doing represents a threat to national security, says Zoulas, of the anti-drug trafficking unit.

There are road accidents, as well as family violence, depressions, suicides and more than 700 overdoses each year, he says, "and it distorts the economy".

"We are heading towards a potential systemic breakdown of the criminal justice system."

DZ Mafia members filmed firing guns from a balcony in Marseille - the footage was then posted on social media

Police have had success in Marseille and other cities against drug gangs.

Criminal operations are being frustrated, more kingpins arrested and drugs seized.

Since 2024, about 1,000 individuals associated with DZ Mafia have been arrested, and 600 incarcerated, police say.

But overall, it is a battle that is becoming more arduous, rather than easier.

As gangs seek to widen their operations, or replace members who have been arrested or killed, social media has become a potent recruitment tool - especially since the Covid pandemic, when many street-drug sales jumped online.

On social media, we found countless posts advertising jobs in Marseille for aspiring drivers, couriers and hitmen, often with glamourised AI-generated images.

Nets are also being cast wider, as it becomes harder to entice young people locally because they know more about what might happen to them.

The ads can reach teenagers in most corners of France - including Paris, Nantes and Lille - and serve as a powerful pull to Marseille, and the Quartier Nord, police say.

Online advertising has also changed the typical profile of someone who would join a gang's ranks, say law enforcement officials.

Now, it is often someone without a criminal past, but who has exploitable vulnerabilities, like having a difficult family life.

"These people are recruited by unscrupulous sponsors who send them into the lion's den," explains Thibaut Fontaine, the head of France's anti-organised-crime unit.

Their lack of criminal experience, he says, makes them easier to arrest quickly.

Karine still has no idea why her son, Hugo, was shot.

Asked what she would say to gang leaders recruiting children and young men in Marseille, she says: "They destroy families. Little sisters, little brothers, grandparents. But then, I don't think those people have a heart, really."

The restaurant where her son said he was going to work did exist, but staï¬ there told Karine they had never recruited anyone called Hugo.

The reason for his death almost certainly lies within Marseille's Quartier Nord.

Additional reporting by Marianne Baisnee, Lea Guedj and Paul Pradier

#107. Samoan Chef Brings Flavors of Home to New Zealand’s Fine-Dining Scene

Food was at the heart of Henry Onesemo’s South Pacific island upbringing. Now, he’s bringing flavors of home to his Michelin-starred restaurant in New Zealand.

#106. ‘I know what it’s like to fight’- France’s most popular sportsman says he’s running for president

Five-times Olympic judo champion Teddy Riner says he is neither leftwing nor rightwing but would seek to ‘bring people together’

The French judo star and five-times Olympic gold medallist Teddy Riner has announced plans to run in next year’s presidential election, describing himself as someone neither leftwing nor rightwing but who would seek to “bring people together”.

With more than five months before the April-May ballot to replace Emmanuel Macron, the race has been shaping up to be a showdown between political extremes. Polls point to a hollowing out of the political centre, suggesting that the anti-immigrant National Rally leader Marine Le Pen could be the frontrunner, with the radical-left leader Jean-Luc Mélenchon trailing behind.

Riner joins a crowded field of candidates, with analysts describing him as a long shot, given his lack of experience and no party backing.

On Monday, he sought to position himself as someone who would unite people and bring “common sense, steadfastness, justice” to France.

“I know what people are going to say about me: ‘He’s crazy. He’s not from the establishment. He doesn’t have the codes. He’s never done politics,’” Riner said in a video posted on social media.

He listed the issues facing the country, from soaring debt to the rising cost of groceries and the precarious futures faced by those who don’t have the right network, family name or skin colour. “If refusing to bow my head and refusing the chaos makes me crazy, then yes, I’m completely crazy,” he said.

The announcement comes about six weeks after Riner, who has often been voted France’s most popular sportsperson, faced a wave of racist abuse and online harassment after telling a TV sports show he felt “anxiety” about the rise of the far right in France.

It also comes as the country has been rocked by protests over the cost of living, heightening the ongoing protests by schoolchildren over crumbling and crowded classrooms.

Riner, 37, who was born in the French Caribbean territory of Guadeloupe and grew up in Paris, cited his years of throwing and pinning down judo opponents to make the case for being elected president. “Fighting has always been part of me. I know what it’s like to fight, fall and always get up,” he said. “France does not need a professional politician who recites lessons and quarrels all day long.”

In an interview with the newspaper Le Parisien published on Monday, he described his views as neither rightwing nor leftwing.

Instead Riner, one of France’s most recognisable and popular athletes, pointed to broader issues. “The country is experiencing deep divisions right now, let’s not kid ourselves,” he said. “And in the face of that, I don’t see a single candidate putting forward a credible and fair vision capable of bringing French people together rather than dividing them. There is no one today offering genuine, sincere hope. It’s always one side against another.”

As Riner prepared to attempt to gather 500 sponsorship signatures from elected officials, as required by French law, he said some of his motivation to run came from those young and old who were finding it difficult to envision a future for themselves.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

“I hear the anger, which some people echo in their rhetoric with a great deal of negativity, coming from people who no longer have any ambition or the desire to think big,” he said. “Yet France is a great country. It allowed me to become the person I am today and gave me the opportunity to dream big. Today, that is precisely what is missing. There is no longer that ambition, no longer that hope.”

If elected, he said he was hoping to strike a balance between both careers so he would be able to compete in the 2028 Los Angeles Olympics.

He wouldn’t be the first judoka to embrace French politics: David Douillet was elected to the French parliament in 2009, years after his retirement from judo, later serving as the sports minister from September 2011 to May 2012.

Dani Podgoretskaya, an analyst at Eurasia Group, said there was little chance Riner would be seen as a serious contender.

“The French take the presidential election very seriously, given how much power the office holds. He has no experience in elected office, no party and no detailed programme yet,” she told Agence France-Presse. “While I still find it possible that we may yet see a providential figure emerge who unites the anti-populist vote, I doubt that Riner is that figure.”

When asked about his priorities on the world stage, Riner seized on the moment to crack a joke about potentially meeting the US president. “A lot of people around me have been talking about my first handshake with Donald Trump,” he said. With a laugh, he added: “We’ll see who has the firmer handshake.”

#105. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

#103. Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Jensen Huang, 63, has been called the 'Taylor Swift of tech' in the US

The tech world has spent the day digesting the extraordinary news that Jensen Huang - the charismatic, leather-jacket wearing chief executive of the multi-trillion dollar firm Nvidia - is being investigated by his own company over an extramarital affair with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy.

None of us had that on our bingo cards for 2026.

Huang's alleged romantic affair with Alice Huang (no relation), the former CEO of a Singapore-based data centre company, has cast ripples far beyond his immediate family - undoubtedly on an emotional rollercoaster of their own - taking us to a crossroads at which technology, geopolitics and national security intersect.

In a nutshell, this is about the bitter rivalry between the US and China, as well as whether Jensen Huang has betrayed the US as well as his wife.

The US is locked in a fierce race with China to develop the best AI models.

"Whoever wins AI, wins," as US President Donald Trump puts it. He thinks that the country with the more dominant AI technology will benefit the most from it and, ultimately, will be the most powerful in the world. He may well be right.

Nvidia happens to make the best chips in the business for AI â so much so that it has been overwhelmed with demand.

It sets its prices accordingly: a single unit of the firm's latest kit for data centres starts at Â£37,000 ($50,000). A medium or large site might have hundreds of them.

The US administration initially slapped an export ban on Nvidia's more recent devices to prevent China from taking advantage of their superior capabilities. It has since softened the rules a little.

But there are suggestions that Alice Huang's company, Megaspeed, may have been involved in sneaking them into the country while she was in charge. Bloomberg reported that the firm bought billions of dollars worth of Nvidia tech in November 2025. Megaspeed denies that it was used illegitimately.

For his part, Jensen Huang has lobbied heavily for the export rules between the two countries to be relaxed, arguing against the weight of general opinion that the AI race doesn't matter and that it would be no bad thing for China to become dependent on US tech.

"President Trump wants American to win and President Xi wants China to win, and it's possible for both of them to do so," he told the BBC last year.

It think it's safe to assume that neither country sees it that way.

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

The difficult questions Nvidia now has to try to answer include whether Alice gained special access to the firm's latest technology, either deliberately or not, via Jensen - and, if so, whether she shared it with Beijing or others.

We don't know when their affair began or ended, but there are numerous photos and videos of Jensen and Alice together at events. It is feasible that there were moments when they spent quite a lot of time together and Jensen has talked about always having an eye on his work.

China denies that she was engaged in espionage.

Interest in economic espionage is neither new nor unique to China. But many Western countries say that China does it on a grand scale, using a combination of technology tools and agents embedded in various organisations - including private companies.

China having access to significant enough numbers of Nvidia's chips to make use of them, or stripping them down and learning how to make its own versions, would infuriate the Trump administration, which is trying to keep its AI technology close to its chest.

Some models have even been withheld from the UK-based AI Safety Institute on the grounds that they are potentially too dangerous to be shared. The US does not want anyone having a headstart or misusing its innovations.

Another potential bombshell lies in the form of any national security information to which Jensen Huang may have been privy.

He is a man operating at the very top of the US elite - one of a handful of people who could probably walk into the White House unannounced and have tea with Trump. He has influence, he has access and he has powerful friends. It's quite possible that secrets were shared with him.

Nvidia says there's no evidence so far that he passed anything on and the firm has launched an internal investigation. Huang says he will co-operate and wants to keep his job - though if nothing else, he has broken Nvidia's own code of conduct, which states that company members should not have relationships with clients.

At present, he has the backing of both the Nvidia board and Trump, who described him as "honourable" and speculated that there must have been a reason for what had transpired. In other words, he's not throwing his tech boss buddy under the bus.

Nvidia's share price has fallen but has not tanked spectacularly, suggesting shareholders are not abandoning the company under his stewardship in their droves either.

An AI industry with a weakened Nvidia would undoubtedly be a slower one.

And there isn't really a natural successor waiting in the wings to replace Jensen were he forced out.

Huang has run Nvidia for more than 30 years - to many, he is Nvidia. He has steered it through multiple crises and it was his decision for the firm to focus on AI before it was trendy.

He is known to be a demanding boss who expects staff to work long hours and to high standards. There are stories about him calling all-staff meetings to tell off individuals who had made mistakes. He has said this was to prevent others making the same errors â but you can only imagine how it felt for those on the receiving end.

While he awaits the conclusion of the investigation into his actions, Huang must hope that, despite not tolerating the mistakes of his colleagues in the past, they will be gracious about his own failings.

And he must also live with the irony that he was potentially caught out not by a sophisticated high-tech sting, but by the oldest trick in the book: a honey trap.

#102. Iran was behind cyber-attack on French supermarket freezers, Paris says

French foreign minister vows European sanctions against Tehran over ‘unprecedented’ hacks last month

France has said Iran was responsible for a massive cyber-attack on supermarket freezers and chiller cabinets that was described by its foreign minister as being an act of “unprecedented scale and unprecedented aggression”.

Jean-Noël Barrot said “Europe-wide sanctions” would be imposed against those responsible, adding that the attacks – which took control of digital thermostats to push temperatures up to above 50C – constituted “a major violation of the nation’s interests”.

At a press briefing during the G7 summit in Paris, he said: “Thanks to the remarkable efforts of government agencies, I am able to confirm that the Islamic Republic of Iran’s Revolutionary Guards are responsible for this attack.”

He said the Iranian ambassador had been summoned “to protest in the strongest possible terms against this extremely serious act.”

Swathes of French supermarkets were hit in the cyber-attacks on 11 and 12 October. Some were forced to throw away entire inventories of perishable food.

Similar incidents have been reported since, including on supermarket fridges in other European countries last week and on a French blood bank.

The Paris public prosecutor, Laure Beccuau, said on Monday that specialist investigators and government agencies had identified “a modus operandi” characteristic of the Iranian group Shahid Kaveh, a cyber warfare unit linked to the Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps (IRGC).

This included “the use of Farsi and IP addresses traced back to devices located in Iran”, she said.

Beccuau said her office had opened a investigation against the head of Shahid Kaveh, his deputies and “others who will be identified” on charges of “fraudulent attacks on an automated data processing system committed as part of an organised crime ring and of causing damage dangerous to persons”.

She said the offences were “aggravated by the fact they were committed to serve a foreign power – in this case Iran”.

Gérôme Billois, a partner at the cybersecurity and technology company Wavestone, said Shahid Kaveh had been active since 2020 and was known to have carried out cyber-attacks mostly on water distribution systems in the US.

“Two countries are known for this kind of attack: Iran and Russia. Given that recent cyber-attacks on supermarkets and the blood bank in France concerned freezers it is reasonable to think Iran is directly linked to both,” he said,

“For the moment they are attacking easy equipment but as a state group that has the means, the determination and the time, we should be prepared to see attacks on other systems like water or electricity.”

During the attack last month, hackers remotely overrode the temperature setting on thousands of freezers, fridges and chiller cabinets in as many as 700 supermarkets and shops across France, pushing them from as low as -27C (-16.6F) to well over 50C (122F) in some cases.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

Thousands of tons of food had to be dumped and in some stores the extreme temperature rise sparked fires, triggering sprinklers and water damage.

Hackers last week accessed control systems at cold stores stocking blood products. The French blood service (EFS) said temperature and defrosting thresholds and been raised to “very high level” but that technicians had been alerted by alarm systems allowing them to disconnect the cold stores before the products were destroyed. It is not clear whether the two attacks are linked.

The following day retailers in 11 European countries including the UK, Belgium, Estonia, Ireland, Finland, Luxembourg, the Netherlands and Sweden reported problems with refrigeration systems.

A regional branch of France’s Crédit Mutuel bank also reported losing more than €100m in a cyber-attack on Friday. The bank said it had recuperated some of the money.

The European Commission did not immediately comment on the French foreign minister’s statement. Kaja Kallas, the head of foreign affairs for the EU said: “We have the foreign affairs council next Monday, so we will have the opportunity to discuss this. These cyber incidents occurring in Europe, if attributed [to Iran], also call for a strong response on our part.”

In a significant decision this year, the EU designated the IRGC a terrorist organisation, ending years of division over the issue. The designation became possible after France dropped its long-held opposition, having previously feared it would damage diplomatic channels and the interests of French nationals in Iran.

#101. The superpower battle over energy – and what it means for your bills

The superpower battle over energy â and what it means for your bills

A gas rig in the North Sea. An oil field in Texas. A solar panel factory in China. They can feel a world away, but your energy bill says otherwise.

In January, it could jump by more than a quarter â around Â£460 extra a year for the typical UK household â according to the latest forecasts from the big suppliers British Gas, EDF and E.ON.

For the second time in four years, a war thousands of miles away is landing in British inboxes.

Since US and Israeli strikes on Iran in February effectively closed the Strait of Hormuz, oil and gas prices have soared.

Winter is expected to push them higher still: demand for gas is rising just as Europe's winter gas stores are at their lowest since records began in 2011.

And there's a bigger threat looming over all of this: the warming climate. Cutting carbon emissions is essential to limit future warming and avoid the most damaging effects of climate change. But keeping bills down while cutting emissions is proving hard â and not just for the UK.

As world leaders gather this week in Antalya, Turkey, for COP31, the UN climate summit, the same tension is playing out around the world. Energy and climate policy has become the front line in a contest between the world's two energy superpowers, leaving everyone else squeezed in between.

America is betting on fossil-fuel molecules: pumping record volumes of oil and gas and pressing its allies to buy them. China is betting on electrons: flooding the world with the solar panels, batteries and electric cars that can replace those fuels.

Whether the bet on fossil fuels or renewables wins will shape not just what the world pays for energy, but how fast it can cut the emissions that are heating the planet.

The UK, which already buys nearly a quarter of its imported gas from America and much of its clean energy kit from China, is being pulled both ways. So which path will it take? Is there a middle way and what will it all mean for your bills?

On the Moray Firth near Inverness, a long spit of sand and dunes curls out into the sea at Whiteness Head. Behind it lies a yard where, for nearly 30 years, thousands of workers built the giant platforms that opened up North Sea oil.

Ming Yang, one of China's biggest wind turbine makers, wanted to bring it back to life with a Â£1.5bn factory and up to 1,500 jobs initially generated. In March, after US lawmakers had urged the UK to block it, ministers did just that, citing national security.

Panorama, Fire and Floods: Our Extreme Weather

Last month, they approved a new North Sea gas field, Jackdaw. No to Chinese wind turbines; yes to North Sea gas.

Few countries are more exposed to the energy shock than the UK. Gas heats most homes and, much of the time, sets the wholesale price of electricity. The last shock, after Russia's invasion of Ukraine, cost households an extra Â£63bn â around Â£2,200 each â according to the Energy and Climate Intelligence Unit and E3G, two climate and energy research organisations.

Wind turbines represent homegrown energy, but they come with potential problems

Less than half of the UK's gas now comes from its own North Sea fields, and the energy we import often comes from places that turn out to be unstable or unreliable.

At the same time, Energy Secretary Miatta Fahnbulleh says tackling climate change remains a priority after Britain's hottest summer on record. The heatwaves, droughts and wildfires it brought would have been almost impossible without global warming, the Met Office's chief scientist told BBC Panorama.

But when I pressed Fahnbulleh on which matters most, bills or climate, she was unequivocal: "With the cost of living biting, affordability is my overriding objective, without a doubt."

"We are far too exposed to global markets we have no control over," Fahnbulleh told me. "What's the solution to that? Homegrown clean energy."

There are two ways to make Britain's energy more homegrown: drill for more of it, or build more of it.

Within hours of Jackdaw's approval, US President Donald Trump - at war with wind turbines since objecting to a wind farm near his Aberdeenshire golf course in 2006 - took to Truth Social. "They should go FULL BLAST!!!" he wrote. "TAKE DOWN THE COSTLY AND RIDICULOUS WINDMILLS!"

Now the UK government faces a tougher choice - whether to approve Rosebank, west of Shetland, the UK's largest undeveloped oil field.

Rosebank is far bigger than Jackdaw - Fahnbulleh has said it would produce about seven times the emissions - and it is mostly oil, not gas. The subject has reportedly split the cabinet. A decision is expected within weeks.

Fahnbulleh, centre, at Jackdaw last month. She says the drilling will have minimal impact on energy prices

The government's opponents are already blaming high bills on its reluctance to drill. Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch has told the prime minister to "stop faffing about" and give permission, calling drilling "the only way we can protect families from rising bills".

"If we use our own gasâ¦ that will bring our bills down," says Reform UK's Richard Tice.

Last week's US midterms were a reminder of the political risks. Soaring petrol prices are widely seen as a key reason Trump's Republicans lost control of Congress.

But when I asked Fahnbulleh whether producing more British oil and gas would bring bills down, she was clear. "No, it doesn't," she said. "In the end, the oil and gas is sold on global markets."

Critics go further. Rosebank "doesn't really do anything for energy security", argues Tessa Khan of the campaign group Uplift, which has fought the field in the courts. "What it does do is send a very dangerous signal about the UK's seriousness about addressing climate change after the summer that we've just had."

Zoom out, though, and the row looks rather different. The North Sea is in long-term decline. Output has fallen by more than two-thirds since its peak in 1999, and the Office for Budget Responsibility expects it to keep falling by around 10% a year â even with Rosebank, which its forecast assumes goes ahead.

Whatever ministers decide, the basin's most productive days are behind it. Jackdaw's reserves amount to only a few months of UK gas demand, while Rosebank contains less than a year's worth of our oil consumption.

The debate around Rosebank and Jackdaw has sparked vehement opposition from climate protesters

And last week the government introduced a bill to write its ban on new exploration licences into law. Rosebank, which is already licensed, is not covered. That could let ministers have it both ways: approve the last big field, while promising there will never be another.

With bills set to rise again in January, that may prove a tempting combination.

Gas is bought, burned and bought again. A wind turbine or solar panel costs money to put up but can then generate electricity for decades without needing shipments of fuel.

The government's climate advisers, the Climate Change Committee, calculate that a future price shock like 2022's would push household energy bills up 59% if Britain stayed on a high-carbon path - but only 4% if it focused on renewables.

The cost of power from new solar farms fell by 90% between 2010 and 2024, and from onshore wind by 70%, according to the International Renewable Energy Agency. But the recent bout of global inflation has been pushing initial costs back up. Last year the Danish developer Ãrsted scrapped plans for Hornsea 4, a giant wind farm off the Yorkshire coast, blaming soaring supply chain costs.

Higher interest rates have made things worse. Most of the cost of a wind or solar farm comes up front, so developers must borrow heavily before they generate any power.

And there is another problem. Britain needs expensive new electricity networks to bring that renewable power to the towns and cities where it is needed. Meanwhile, gas-fired stations still frequently set wholesale power prices - meaning any cost savings are not always reflected in customer bills.

All of which means building more renewables does not automatically mean lower household bills.

And there is another issue - China's dominance of clean tech. It makes the vast majority of the world's solar panels and batteries, and around two-thirds of its electric cars.

And China's lead is growing. In March, its exports of solar panels, batteries and electric cars hit a record, up 70% on a year earlier, according to the think tank Ember.

The UK wants to escape the fossil fuels that leave households exposed to price shocks and keep adding to global warming. Much of the technology needed to replace those fuels comes from China - a country it is increasingly reluctant to depend on.

I asked Fahnbulleh whether Britain was swapping dependence on petrostates for dependence on Beijing. "Well, look, we are," she said, adding: "we've got a diversified global market".

Britain will keep buying from China where it needs to, says Fahnbulleh, but she wants more of the kit made here. "I want to see turbines," she said.

Yet UK-built turbines were exactly what Ming Yang was offering.

"There was a security risk, and national security comes first and foremost," says Fahnbulleh. The government has never publicly explained what that risk was.

Two days before Jackdaw was approved, Japan's Sumitomo Electric opened Britain's first HVDC subsea cable factory at Nigg, a short way up the coast from Whiteness Head, creating up to 250 jobs.

Foreign investment, then - just not from China.

Earlier this year the US formally left the Paris climate agreement and is not expected to send a delegation to this year's COP. Without American pressure, others have less reason to make bold promises: national pledges now add up to a cut in global emissions of just 6% by 2035, the UN says â a tenth of what scientists say is needed to limit warming to 1.5C.

Donald Trump is a big exponent of fossil fuels

China is beginning to fill the space. Li Shuo, of the Asia Society Policy Institute, calls it a "role reversal". Once "a very conservative player" at climate talks, China now sees pushing the global climate agenda as serving its national interest â and, he says, "we're only at the beginning of this". It aims to hit one of COP31's flagship goals, getting 35% of its energy from electricity, five years early.

And increasingly it is Chinese companies, rather than governments' promises, that are driving the transition. "It's really just a China story," says Li Shuo.

If that is right, it leaves the UK in an uncomfortable place. Hard choices â and they come when household budgets are already stretched thin.

Thatcher's Right to Buy created a generation of homeowners - did it also fuel the housing crisis?

The NHS is relying more on charities - and that might be creating problems for patients

#100. Cyber-attack on French supermarket freezers has geopolitical context

‘Not particularly complex’ defrosting hack has been attributed to Iran, with presumption it is linked to others across Europe

In 2023 a state-backed group interrupted TV streaming services in the United Arab Emirates to broadcast a deepfake report on the war in Gaza. In the same year, hackers affiliated with Iran displayed the slogan “down with Israel” on the screens of water-control equipment made by an Israeli firm.

Now France has attributed an attack on supermarket fridges to hackers operating under the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, a key branch of Iran’s military establishment.

There was no message this time, but the disruption made headlines around the world. Some refrigerators had their temperature turned up so high they caught fire. ‌In the hours after the hack, supermarket employees dumped piles of perished meat and melted ice-cream into rubbish containers.

France’s foreign minister described it as an act of “unprecedented scale and unprecedented aggression” and promised “Europe-wide sanctions” would be imposed against those responsible.

It follows attacks on industrial freezers at an Israeli food production plant in May and a wave of freezer malfunctions that spoiled food at US military bases in August that are now reportedly being treated as a suspected hack. Those attacks have not been officially attributed to Iran, but the French announcement is the first to implicate the state in this type of hack.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

Rafe Pilling, a senior director at Sophos, a cybersecurity firm, said the relatively unsophisticated nature of the attack – hackers used default passwords to access fridge controls – was in line with Iranian capabilities. “The attacks themselves were not particularly complex,” he said.

Pilling said there was now a presumption the French attacks and a wave of rogue defrosting hacks across Europe last week were linked.

Hostile cyber operations against the west tend to come from a vexatious quartet of China, Russia, Iran and North Korea. All have different aims and characteristics: China has focused on industrial espionage and mass data gathering, Russia on disrupting enemy infrastructure and spreading disinformation, and North Korea on hacking crypto exchanges because it needs money. Iran, according to Pilling, wants to put a shot across the bow of its enemies.

“In general Iran uses cyber-attacks to project power internationally and to signal their political displeasure with regional and international adversaries,” Pilling said.

The fridge hacks came as the UK’s national cybersecurity centre warned the country is in the “foothills” of a new level of cyber threat – and should expect a “huge rise” in the volume of incidents. The main concern is AI, a technology now capable of highlighting previously unforeseen flaws in IT systems and helping attackers plan and carry out attacks.

However, the underlying context of the fridge attacks is not AI but geopolitics. After all, the refrigeration system vulnerabilities were relatively easy to find and exploit. But a combination of groundbreaking technology and tense global relations makes for an alarming landscape.

#98. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#96. ‘Settle down’ and ‘do your own swabs’: how alleged rape victims are treated in Queensland revealed in major report

Prosecutors dropped cases because survivors ‘didn’t act how a rape victim should’, Australian Institute of Criminology review of 19,000 sexual assault cases finds

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Queensland police put sexual assault investigations “on the backburner” because detectives were overworked and under pressure to prioritise cases linked to political issues such as youth crime, a major new study has found.

The report, published this month by the Australian Institute of Criminology, sought to investigate why so many sexual assault complaints – about two-thirds of those made to police in Queensland – were withdrawn, not substantiated or considered unsolved.

Interviews with detectives and others paint a grim picture about the experience of making a sexual assault complaint. One told the researchers that police interviews with victim-survivors were often conducted in “the room where the admin ladies sit”.

“Previous to that we used the ladies’ change room,” the officer said.

Another said: “We have victims of rapes coming into this office with other officers here answering phones.”

Detectives told researchers they were struggling to cope with heavy workloads and faced pressure to respond to matters that were considered political priorities, including youth crime, property crime and domestic and family violence.

“The serious jobs like sexual assault, they’re life changing, life-ruining stuff,” one detective said.

“And they’re not getting any attention at all because some kid walked into a bottle shop and stole a bottle of vodka, and we end up dealing with that instead.”

Another said: “So you take a statement from them, and then something will pop up where someone’s running around with a gun, so now that’s your focus.

“A few jobs like this back up, and then all of a sudden … it’s been 12 months.”

Investigators told the researchers that cases, particularly relating to historical sexual offence allegations, had been “sitting there for yonks” and “get put on the backburner because there’s just too much other work”.

Officers spoke about being in a state of permanent “stress response” because of the intense workload.

“When we’re in a stress response, we don’t act with our compassion systems in place; neurobiologically, your empathy system is offline,” an officer said. “The police are under so much stress and then we’re asking them to perform in a trauma informed victim-centric way.”

The researchers found that officers who investigated sexual assault offences were often generalist detectives required to also handle property crime or complex fraud cases. Their data showed that when investigations were handled by officers who had specialist training in sexual offences, case attrition rates decreased.

Some sexual assault support service workers raised concerns about police responses in interviews with researchers.

One spoke about a victim-survivor who “presented very heightened to police [and] basically was told settle down or we’ll arrest you”.

Detectives described difficulties obtaining rape kit forensic tests at public hospitals, including cases where they had to drive victim-survivors up to seven hours for a test, or where qualified hospital staff were not available to conduct tests.

“We’ve got a hospital that is literally 250 metres from the police station,” one officer said. “If somebody turns up at 2am reporting a rape, you’re not getting them back here until at least 9am in the morning.”

Another said: “We’ve been told there is someone on call 24 hours every day of the year to do the kits locally. But they’re not picking up the phone.”

In one case recounted by a detective, a victim-survivor waited 14 hours in an emergency ward and was then told to “do their own swabs”.

Detectives working on sexual assault cases also expressed frustration about the decisions of prosecutors not to proceed with cases in court; they said the DPP did not have the same accountability measures as the police.

“The DPP get rid of cases by saying ‘I don’t believe what she said because they didn’t act how a rape victim should’,” one officer said.

Another said: “Victim trust is affected when they’re reassured by police that it’s a good case, and then the DPP decides no.”

The study looked at more than 19,000 sexual assault allegations made to police between 2018 and 2022. It found that only 36% were considered “solved”. Of the remainder, 31% were “withdrawn by the victim-survivor”, 11% were deemed by police to be not substantiated, and 22% considered “unsolved”.

The chief executive of the Queensland Sexual Assault Network (Qsan), Angela Lynch, said data showing nearly one-third of sexual violence cases were withdrawn was “very concerning” and pointed to “larger systemic issues and barriers for victims”.

“These issues unfortunately are consistent with frontline sexual violence worker and victim-survivor experience. Delays in the system are a huge impediment to victim-survivors being able to continue to engage and these, along with other issues, can leave [them] with an impression their case is not a priority.”

She said Qsan supported a specialist response to sexual violence, including by police.

The authors of the new report – titled Attrition of sexual offences in police investigations: Exploring police, case and system factors that lead to withdrawn and unfounded outcomes – made a number of recommendations, including that police should “invest in specialist domestic, family and sexual violence investigation units”.

The Queensland police service recently closed its specialist domestic and family violence and vulnerable persons command.

The report researchers also argued that general duties officers should receive “foundational” training about sexual offences. They said officers and prosecutors should be required to advise victim-survivors personally when a case is not proceeding to prosecution.

The Queensland police service was contacted and offered the opportunity to comment.

Information and support for anyone affected by rape or sexual abuse is available at Full Stop Australia (1800 385 578). In the UK, Rape Crisis offers support on 0808 500 2222. In the US, Rainn offers support on 800-656-4673. Other international helplines can be found here

#92. ‘Exceedingly heavy-handed’: ACT police criticised by magistrate for Tasering disabled Aboriginal woman

Exclusive: Body-worn camera footage shows woman pulled off bench and a Taser activated despite ‘putting up no resistance’, magistrate says

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A magistrate has criticised “exceedingly heavy-handed” ACT police officers who Tasered an Aboriginal woman with an intellectual disability during a routine arrest.

The woman, named Emma, pleaded with officers not to use the stun gun on her and told them she was unarmed during the arrest at a Canberra bus stop in January.

Police were called because of reports Emma had a knife, but she was only found to have two pairs of scissors, one of which was broken, in a nearby backpack.

The court was shown police body-worn footage of the arrest, in which she is seen raising her hands immediately after officers arrived and pointed a Taser towards her.

As she pushed back against an officer who was trying to handcuff her she was pulled to the ground.

While two officers held her down another placed a Taser against her back and activated it, in what is known as “drive stun” mode. Seven officers were present at the time.

The ACT magistrates court heard in September that officers also threatened to use OC spray against Emma after she appeared to self-harm by deliberately striking her head against a police van during the arrest.

A transcript of the hearing was released by the court to Guardian Australia in October.

Special magistrate Sean Richter, a former South Australian police officer, said he found the conduct of officers “incredibly” and “exceedingly” heavy-handed, and agreed that they were aware of who Emma was and her vulnerabilities before they arrested her.

“I sometimes wonder when we lost the ability to speak to people,” Richter said.

“She’s a woman that was putting up no resistance.

“She’s sitting on the bench with her arms in the air.”

Richter said Emma was involved in a motor vehicle accident when she was 12 and received a traumatic brain injury.

He said the ACT civil and administrative tribunal described her as having a mental impairment and a neurocognitive disorder, and said she had been placed on a psychiatric treatment order.

Emma was charged with multiple offences by police in relation to the incident, but all of them except possessing a knife without reasonable excuse were withdrawn by the prosecution. She pleaded guilty to that offence, which related to the scissors in her bag, and was convicted. Emma also faced Richter on other matters unrelated to the arrest.

“I was shown the video of her arrest on the charge of possess knife without reasonable excuse. I will say this; I consider that the actions of the police in that matter were unwarranted and heavy-handed,” Richter said.

“Here was a woman sitting on a park bench putting her arms in the air. She’s clearly known to the police because one of them names her.

“It’s blatantly apparent to anyone that deals with this woman that she has a significant cognitive disorder and should be treated as such. I find it amazing that in this day and age the police reacted in the way that they did.”

In a statement, ACT policing said it “notes the comments of Special Magistrate Richter in relation to the arrest of a woman in January 2026.

“As an associated civil claim is under way in relation to this matter, it would not be appropriate to provide further comment.”

In that civil claim filed in July, Emma’s lawyer, Michael Lalor, accused one of the officers involved in the arrest of misfeasance in public office.

The officer is accused of making a series of misleading statements or omissions in police evidence that resulted in more serious charges being laid, and Emma spending 28 days in custody before bail was granted after her lawyers viewed the body-worn camera footage of the arrest.

The statement of claim alleges the misleading statements include that Emma ignored police commands, that she had “abruptly” bent down towards her bag, that she attempted to strike an officer multiple times in the face, and that she was Tasered because she engaged in a “high level of violence”.

Lalor also writes in the civil claim that it would be alleged police provided misleading statements regarding the witness who called officers saying they had seen a woman with a knife.

Police said that the civilian witness had “immediately feared for his safety and departed swiftly” after seeing Emma, but the statement of claim said police failed to make clear the man and his partner “were inside a moving vehicle with the doors locked at the relevant time”.

That witness also told police, according to the statement of claim, that “I wasn’t particularly scared, I was in a car with the doors locked, so I knew she couldn’t really stab me.”

Lalor is also alleging breaches of the Human Rights Act and exemplary damages for assault and battery.

Police have not yet been required to file a defence to the claim.

ACT police have come under repeated recent scrutiny for their use of force, particularly against Aboriginal people.

In August, the commonwealth and ACT ombudsman criticised police for failing to clearly define “racial profiling” after officers in Canberra pulled guns on an Indigenous teenager they mistook for a suspect in an aggravated burglary.

In June last year, the ACT ombudsman found that in about a third of cases it reviewed, unprofessional behaviour by officers such as impatience, aggression, foul and abusive language appeared to unnecessarily escalate situations.

This led to “use of force in circumstances where a more sensitive and trauma-informed approach to negotiation and de-escalation might have avoided the need to use force or as much force”.

As part of that investigation, the ombudsman recommended that police identify and implement measures to better equip its officers with a greater understanding of “mental illness and how it affects a person’s behaviour and ability to comply with directions”, but police did not accept the recommendation.

#87. High fossil fuel prices spread inflation ‘like a cancer’, UN climate chief tells Cop31

Simon Stiell makes opening address at summit in Turkey, where talks are under way about speeding up shift to electrification

A global fossil fuel price crisis is “spreading inflation like a cancer” around the planet, the UN’s climate chief, Simon Stiell, told the opening plenary of critical UN climate talks in Turkey.

Stiell joined Cop31 hosts Turkey and Australia, which is leading the negotiations, in promoting renewable energy and a new electrification goal as an optimistic alternative to a planet still tied to oil, coal and gas.

Inside the tight security of the opening plenary on Monday, delegates from almost 200 countries watched a stirring short film with scenes of fire, flood and storms that proclaimed: “It’s time to reconnect with the planet we have grown apart from.”

Turkey said it expected 100,000 visitors, including national delegations, to attend the two-week talks, including at least 95 heads of state. A global leaders summit is scheduled for Wednesday and Thursday. But the talks are under increased pressure to deliver, after growing dissatisfaction with the snail’s pace of results.

The talks are opening as a historic El Niño builds in the tropical Pacific and months after a glacier collapse set off catastrophic flooding in Nepal and Tibet, wiping away villages and killing at least 1,459 people, with thousands more still missing. Climate scientists confirmed this year that global heating would reach at least 1.8C – beyond the 1.5C temperature goal that countries promised to aim for at talks in Paris in 2015.

Last week Johan Rockström, the director of the Potsdam Institute for Climate Impact Research, told the Guardian that although he still had faith in the negotiating process, “the fact that global warming is accelerating, that we inevitably will be breaching the 1.5C limit within three to five years, that tipping points are coming closer fast … and that this is allowed to happen within the framework of the Paris agreement, cannot be considered as anything but a global failure.”

And in a statement the UN secretary general, António Guterres, urged leaders to “get serious by cutting emissions faster, accelerating a just transition from fossil fuels and delivering the finance and protection vulnerable countries need”.

Stiell also said: “The urgency of this moment must drive us forward. Global heating is already costing the Earth,” adding that it was vulnerable communities paying the price and frustration at a lack of fairness was palpable.

US president Donald Trump’s war with Iran and the disruption of the key strait of Hormuz supply route for oil pushed up petrol prices around the world by 50%, raising inflation and slowing economic growth.

Stiell told delegates this “fossil fuel cost chaos” had “delivered a global shock wave, hitting households hard and spreading inflation like a cancer”.

Australia’s climate change minister, Chris Bowen, the president of the negotiations, said in a speech that will set the tone for the talks that although the 1.5C Paris target will be overshot “we can return … if we work together and act with urgency, solidarity and resolve”.

With fists clenched in the final moments of a speech, Bowen urged delegates to show future generations that this generation was “equal to the challenge” and to “rise to this moment together”.

The countries pushing for the greatest action at Cop31 have stressed the 1.5C temperature rise limit can’t be abandoned and called for accelerated steps to provide finance to the places most vulnerable to climate impacts. The so-called high-ambition coalition said it has 20 country members pushing for major progress in Antalya. “That limit is in serious danger but overshoot should never become an excuse for inaction, every fraction of a degree matters,” said Jess Gasper, an environmental official for the Marshall Islands.

“We must limit overshoot and return to a safe temperature as quickly as possible.” Gasper said it took eight years to get international finance to build a single sea wall to help protect the Marshall Islands, a Pacific island nation acutely threatened by sea level rise.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

“That example shows we need that money to adapt,” he said. “I refuse to accept that we experience displacement because the world fails to act.”

The talks began without a fight over the agenda, which was considered by many to be a good sign. There was confidence among delegates that a final decision from Cop31 would include reference to a new voluntary electrification goal being championed by the host, Turkey, and the president of the negotiations, Australia.

Bowen said he had spoken to countries around the globe that were represented at the talks and there was “enormous momentum” among them for electrification.

The goal, he said, was to raise the percentage of final energy consumption coming from electricity from the current one-fifth to one-third in the next ten years.

Dr Fatih Birol, the executive director of the International Energy Agency, said: “I have talked with many governments and I see there is a great appetite for many countries to be part of this historic decision [in Antalya].”

He told the Guardian: “The important thing is that we are entering the age of electricity and this [electrification goal] would give an extra boost to that movement.”

Countries agreed at the 2023 climate summit to triple renewable energy capacity by 2030. Birol said if countries endorsed the 2035 electrification target it would give an “unmistakable signal to markets” and a chance to increase both renewable and nuclear electricity.

#86. Watch: Rare insight into France's drug gangs recruiting children to commit violence

A BBC investigation has found one of Europe's most powerful drug gangs in France is recruiting teenage children from across the country to commit kidnap and murder.

Known as the "DZ Mafia", the gang won a bloody turf war against a rival group in Marseille during 2023, and is now recruiting from further afield.

One member who is in prison awaiting trial after being implicated in several murders told the BBC he was part of a DZ Mafia hit squad.

He agrees the killings he has been charged with were "quite shocking".

"Attempts in broad daylight, in traffic jamsâ¦ it can range from going to hit a guy, slap him to taking him in front of his mother and burning him up in a car. I think I have good composure, I handle it fairly well."

When challenged over whether he feels regret when carrying out such acts of brutality, he says he feels "guilty in-part" but claims to have no regrets.

Like so many teenagers from Marseille, he knew large sums of money could be earned quickly in drug trafficking. Now in his twenties, he admits the environment he entered was a "trap".

The BBC's Paris Correspondent, James Waterhouse, reports from Marseille.

#85. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”
