#44. Trial date of alleged Charlie Kirk killer set for June 2028

Kirk was a prominent conservative youth activist

The trial of Tyler Robinson, the man accused of killing conservative activist Charlie Kirk, will start in June 2028, a judge has ordered.

Robinson, 23, has pleaded not guilty to aggravated murder and six other charges in connection with the September 2025 killing of Kirk, 31, who was shot while he spoke at a Utah college campus.

Utah County judge Tony Graf Jr on Monday told the court that jury selection would begin on 21 June, 2028. The prosecution has estimated the trial could last 17 weeks, while defence lawyers have said it could take seven months.

Robinson could face the death penalty if found guilty of aggravated murder, something his defence team has sought to prevent.

Prosecutors previously told the court they could be ready for trial by October 2027 but were willing to move that date back to January 2028 to avoid the holiday period.

But defence attorneys said they needed more time to prepare their case and floated a 2029 start. As well as trying to stop Robinson facing the death penalty, they have also tried to have cameras banned from the courtroom.

Kirk's widow, Erika, with whom he had two children, had pushed for as early a trial date as possible while also arguing proceedings should be live-streamed. She, along with Kirk's parents, â Robert and Kathryn, have attended a number of court hearings in the case.

Robinson's legal team is trying to get the death penalty taken off the table in his trial

At the end of Monday's proceedings, Erica Kirk's lawyer, Jeffrey A Nieman, told the judge that while "a trial in the summer of 2028, three years after Charlie's assassination, does not feel very speedy to the Kirk family", they had "no choice but obviously to accept the schedule the court's put forward".

Nieman added: "The family has waited long enough already; they should not have to wait any longer for this."

During a five-day preliminary hearing in July, prosecutors outlined their evidence to show probable cause for trial.

This included DNA allegedly tying Robinson to the murder weapon and other items recovered at the scene; confessions he allegedly made to loved ones; and a taped video statement from his roommate and romantic partner, Lance Twiggs.

"There is a mountain of evidence that proves that he is the shooter," prosecutor Ryan McBride told the court in September.

"We know that the defendant is the shooterâ¦ first, because of the video evidence; second, because of the DNA; third, because of his admissions; and fourth, because of circumstantial evidence," McBride said.

Defence attorneys, meanwhile, have cast doubt upon evidence and expert witness credentials in the case.

#43. Nvidia opens investigation after report CEO Jensen Huang was target of foreign intelligence

CEO reportedly ‌had close relationship ‌with a Chinese businesswoman who passed information to ‌Beijing

Nvidia said ⁠on Sunday it “is initiating an independent investigation in light of recent reporting that founder and CEO Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort”.

The New York Times reported in ​October that Donald Trump had been briefed last year on intelligence that Huang ‌had a close relationship ‌with a Chinese businesswoman who passed information to ‌Beijing.

Speaking to reporters on Sunday, Trump downplayed the revelations about Huang, who is an influential adviser to the president on artificial intelligence, saying: “I think he’s an honorable guy. I think he probably has an excuse for it.”

Trump also brushed off a question about whether he now regrets not acting on the intelligence when he was briefed on it, and suggested that he would be happy to take Huang with him on a trip to Asia next week. “I haven’t heard he’s done anything wrong,” Trump said. “He dealt with somebody. But, you know, so does everybody else.”

Republican lawmakers have expressed more alarm, asking Huang to give the House foreign affairs and intelligence committees a closed-door briefing next week on Nvidia’s “security protocols against foreign adversary espionage” and his relationship with Alice Huang or Huang Le, an executive at a Singapore-based datacenter company called Megaspeed, who is of no relation to the Nvidia CEO. She had previously been suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China, prompting American officials to surveil and monitor her.

Those officials have determined that she was acting as a Chinese intelligence asset, passing commercial information to Beijing, the New York Times reported, citing unnamed sources.

The company’s board said in a statement on Sunday that, while it has no reason to believe that either US national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised, its inquiry will examine all available information.

“The Board prioritizes US national and economic security, which is paramount to the interests of NVIDIA and its shareholders,” the company said. “The Board has confidence in Jensen and his leadership team and will provide further updates as appropriate.”

Huang fully ‌supports the review and has committed to cooperating with it, it added.

Axios reported on ​Sunday, citing a draft email by Huang to employees, that Huang admitted to having a romantic relationship with a suspected Chinese spy and took full ⁠responsibility for his actions.

“I was unfaithful to my ​wife. My ​actions hurt her ​and our children. There is no excuse ​for what ‌I did,” ​Huang said ​in the draft email also seen by Reuters.

Nvidia did not immediately respond to a request for comment on the Axios report.

The Times reported on Sunday that a memo drafted by US secretary of state Marco Rubio circulated among top policymakers in the Trump administration last spring, citing intelligence that the businesswoman who had a relationship with Nvidia’s chief executive was a spy for China’s ministry of state security. Despite those concerns, Huang was invited to join Trump’s delegation when he traveled to China in May. Huang and his wife, Lori Huang, were also invited to a state dinner at the White House in September for Xi Jinping and seated at the top table with Trump and China’s president.

Nvidia’s powerful chips serve as a cornerstone of the AI boom, placing Jensen Huang into one of the most influential leadership positions in the industry. In that position, he has developed a close partnership with Trump as the US government attempts to defend its position as a leader in AI development.

However, national security concerns have prompted strict US export controls, effectively barring the company from selling its top-tier processors to customers in China.

US officials have cited threats to the US supply chain and fears over national security risks as a rationale for the restrictions. Scott Bessent, the US treasury secretary, has also suggested that China could be sanctioned for theft of US data and intellectual property.

The Trump administration has imposed restrictions on exports of advanced AI chips to China as a matter of national security. Megaspeed’s Alice Huang was suspected to have been helping the government get around those restrictions.

The investigation also follows a rough few days on Wall Street for AI tech firms, particularly Nvidia. A sell-off of tech stocks that began earlier this month and gained steam with disappointing earnings led to a total decline of nearly $1tn in value off the tech-heavy Nasdaq exchange in one week and erased about $2tn in a month.

A drop of 3.1% for Nvidia, previously worth about $5tn, whose chips have made it the poster child of the AI frenzy, was the heaviest force pulling the S&P 500 downward recently.

Nick Robins-Early and Blake Montgomery contributed reporting

#39. Judge denies motion to dismiss Lindsay Clancy's murder case

The BBC's Ione Wells on how the Lindsay Clancy trial gripped America - and ultimately fell apart

A Massachusetts judge has denied a motion to dismiss the murder case against Lindsay Clancy, the US woman accused of killing her three children.

The judge's ruling follows her defence team's request to dismiss the case after the 12-person jury in her September trial failed to reach a verdict.

Clancy has not denied killing her children in 2023, but her defence team argues she was suffering from severe postpartum psychosis at the time. Prosecutors say the killings were intentional.

The ruling clears the way for prosecutors to pursue the case. They could reduce the charges in a potential second trial or dismiss them altogether.

Clancy's defence team argued to dismiss the case against her due to double jeopardy - a legal standard that prevents a person from being tried for the same crime twice.

Her lawyers argued that Judge William Sullivan, who presided over the 26-day jury trial last summer, acted prematurely when he declared a mistrial on 4 September, saying that he should have sought a viable alternative.

The five-week trial, its lead-up and aftermath gripped Americans, and put postpartum mental health issues such as psychosis in the spotlight.

In his ruling on Monday, Sullivan said that a retrial would not unconstitutionally subject Clancy to double jeopardy, according to court documents obtained by the BBC.

The possibility that "an unreasonably stubborn or eccentric juror" could be seated is "an unavoidable risk of the jury system", he said.

"Because the court concludes that there was manifest necessity for a mistrial based on the jury's deadlock, a retrial on the murder indictments does not violate Clancy's right not to be placed in double jeopardy."

Sullivan also denied a defence motion to see records of the lone holdout juror in the case and to question the juror under oath.

He said the defence failed to demonstrate that the records were needed for Clancy's defence at any retrial, and that questioning the juror "would have been impermissibly coercive".

The judge has marked 16 November to discuss scheduling a potential second trial. He previously suggested it could begin in March.

The BBC has contacted Clancy's lawyers for comment.

Lindsay Clancy's ex-husband says he forgave her so he would not be 'eaten up inside forever'

Lindsay Clancy's trial puts postpartum psychosis in the spotlight

Prosecutors in the closely watched case alleged that on 24 January 2023, Clancy intentionally strangled all of her children - Cora, 5, Dawson, 3, and Callan, eight months - in their Duxbury, Massachusetts, home.

After weeks of emotional testimony, the jury took a week to deliberate, repeatedly telling the judge that they could not reach a verdict.

The deadlock stemmed from a lone juror who voted to convict Clancy of first-degree murder, while the 11 other jurors intended to acquit Clancy by reason of insanity.

In Massachusetts, as well as in all US criminal murder trials, juries must reach a unanimous decision to convict or acquit a defendant.

Because the jurors failed to unanimously agree on whether the prosecution had proved Clancy was guilty "beyond a reasonable doubt" as required by US law, the judge declared a mistrial on 4 September, which means that she was neither found guilty nor not guilty.

Clancy is currently being housed in a state-run psychiatric hospital and medical facility in Massachusetts.

#36. Turkey steel factory blast kills at least 17 people

A fire broke out at the factory following the explosion which some eyewitnesses confused with an earthquake

At least 17 people have died and 12 others have been injured in an explosion at an iron and steel factory near the port city of Izmir in western Turkey.

All those killed and injured were workers at the Habas Demir Celik plant in Aliaga, one of the country's leading steel producers, authorities said. Footage from the scene showed a large fire inside the facility and a towering column of smoke rising into the air.

The local governor said the blast happened around midday after a crane carrying a replacement boiler collapsed, causing materials to fall into a furnace below.

Workplace accidents remain a chronic problem in Turkey, the Turkish Health and Safety Labor Watch, an independent civil society organisation, said.

President Recep Tayyip ErdoÄan originally said the death toll was 16, but the Health Minister, Kemal MemiÅoÄlu, later announced that one of those who had been critically injured had later died, taking the toll to 17.

Eyewitnesses at the scene said the explosion shook the ground, with one person initially believing an earthquake had struck.

In a statement, the company that runs the factory said the accident occurred during scheduled maintenance work on one of the plant's electric arc furnaces.

Izmir Governor SÃ¼leyman Elban told media that some people were believed to still be under the rubble.

Speaking after a cabinet meeting on Monday, ErdoÄan confirmed the deaths and said investigations were under way, while search and rescue operations continued.

At least five people have been arrested, according BBC News's Turkish service, citing local media reports.

The Habas plant is in the Izmir district of Aliaga which is a major port and one of Turkey's main shipbreaking hubs.

#35. Ousted Paraguay president under house arrest after banking crisis leaves 60% of country unable to access accounts

Santiago Peña was detained minutes after he was impeached by Congress, as protesters took to the streets demanding answers after the suspension of the Ueno bank

Paraguay’s rightwing president has been placed under house arrest minutes after he was impeached by Congress, amid a severe crisis that left more than half the country’s population unable to access their bank accounts.

Santiago Peña is being investigated about allegations he used his political influence to favour a bank that expanded exponentially under his government and was suspended this week after irregularities.

The move froze the accounts of 2.9 million people, about 60% of the country’s adult population, who were customers of the bank – Ueno - and who overnight could no longer use their own money even for basic purchases such as bread and water.

Protests spread across the country, some involving violent clashes with police. Impeachment proceedings began on Wednesday before being concluded on Friday, minutes before Peña’s arrest was ordered.

His vice-president, Pedro Alliana, was being sworn in as the country’s new leader while a public prosecutor, accompanied by police outside Peña’s home, told local media the former president had been placed under house arrest.

“It has been established that he is indeed inside his home. He voluntarily allowed officers to enter and made himself available for all procedural purposes,” said prosecutor Elena Fiore Franco.

The public prosecutor’s office had opened an investigation into Peña on Thursday on suspicion of illicit enrichment, money laundering and influence peddling.

Peña, who was one of US president Donald Trump’s main allies in the region, had tried to resign on Thursday in an attempt to retain the special jurisdiction afforded to former presidents – who become senators for life in Paraguay – but lawmakers did not accept his resignation, leaving him subject to the ordinary courts.

Amid rumours he had fled to Argentina, the 47-year-old economist was expected to appear before Congress to present his defence during the impeachment proceedings, but left that to his lawyers and instead continued posting on social media.

Before the impeachment was concluded or the arrest warrant issued, he posted: “I say goodbye with gratitude, while fully recognising the anguish of those who are now waiting for answers,” referring to former Ueno customers who are unable to access their own money.

Soon after taking office, Peña appointed Carlos Carvallo Spalding, a former CEO of Ueno, head of the country’s central bank. Three months later, Spalding – who was by now regulating the company he used to run – allowed Ueno to become a digital bank, which put it on the path to becoming one of Paraguay’s largest banks by deposits in just a few years.

The arrest warrant for Peña also includes Spalding.

Peña himself had about US$1m in shares he bought in 2019 in a company that would become Ueno Holding in 2022, the parent company of the conglomerate that controlled Ueno Bank. Even after becoming president, he only disposed of the shares in April 2025. He said he had bought them purely as an “investor”.

As the bank aggressively expanded through an easy account-opening system and by promising returns far above those offered by the rest of the market, it also managed to bring public pension funds controlled by Peña’s administration into its portfolio.

“Because Ueno had the blessing of the presidency, most of the pension funds close to the government went to deposit their money in the bank,” said political analyst Hugo Duarte. An estimated $800m to $860m in state and pension funds were invested in Ueno.

As pressure mounted and retail sales fell by as much as 80% because much of the population could not access their own money, Peña lost the support of his own party.

During the impeachment session, opposition senator Rafael Filizzola said Paraguay was facing “one of the biggest corruption scandals since the return of democracy” and the “biggest fraud carried out from political power in the democratic era”.

#32. Banana producers in Latin America want better pay to cope with effects of climate crisis

Farmers face big drop in production from extreme weather during this year’s super El Niño event

Banana producers in Latin America are calling for better pay to help cope with the impact of the climate crisis, as they face big drops in production resulting from extreme weather during this year’s super El Niño event.

High winds in parts of Colombia, one of the biggest suppliers to the UK market where droughts are predicted in some regions this year, and heavy rainfall in Peru and Ecuador are already affecting production, according to the Fairtrade Foundation ethical trade body. In the Dominican Republic, farmers are expected to face drought conditions, with forecasts of drier and warmer than usual conditions.

Fairtrade research suggests that during the previous El Niño cycle, productivity across its farmers and workers dropped by 13% in the Dominican Republic, 22% in Ecuador, and 6% in Peru compared with the year before. The El Niño event in 1983 – one of the strongest ever recorded – affected 15% of total planted banana area in Ecuador, with a reduction in exports of 36%.

Dr Carley-Jane Stanton, a climate and environment lead at Fairtrade, says this El Niño is expected to lead to more extreme weather, including heavy rainfall and strong winds and “we expect the impacts for producers to be more severe” than in the 23-24 cycle.

Evidence is mounting to fuel those fears. Exports from the Colombian Caribbean region fell 32% to $122m (£91m) between July and September this year, according to the Association of Banana Growers of Magdalena and La Guajira, as reported by the trade publication Fruitnet.

It said that in September alone, shipments were down 42% year on year, with earnings close to halving to $40m from $70m.

“Given the risks threatening the continuity of banana production, we are calling on international markets to foster commercial conditions that recognise actual production costs and contribute to the sector’s sustainability,” the association said.

It said that climate-related productivity losses and a decline in earnings from exports made worse by currency issues was “undermining the economic sustainability of the industry”.

Ever Antonio Flores Muños, a worker at the Agrosiete banana producer in Colombia, told Fairtrade: “Over the past 10 years the climate has changed a lot. It’s been getting much hotter … it’s really intense. Some places have had losses due to the climate, flooding, strong winds. It’s going to affect all of us – our workers, colleagues – everything will be at great risk, and our wages will decrease.”

Fairtrade is calling for legislation in the UK to tackle unfair trading practices, such as low or late payments to farmers and contracts which push risk down the supply chain, so that producers can invest more into improving their crops’ resilience to severe weather and more likelihood of pests and diseases. Worker health is also expected to be affected by more extreme weather.

While some businesses, including Sainsbury’s, the Co-op and Waitrose, have taken voluntary steps by sourcing only Faitrade bananas, which guarantee a minimum price, it says there is more work to do.

Eleanor Harrison, the chief executive of Fairtade, said: “The UK’s banana supply depends on the people growing our bananas having the resources they need to adapt to climate change, and to invest in their future. Fair prices, climate resilience and decent work go hand in hand.”

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

While bananas are becoming harder and more expensive to produce, supermarket prices have remained unchanged for almost 40 years despite rising costs for fertiliser, fuel and labour.

Caoimhe Buckley, the chief corporate affairs officer at the large supplier Fyffes, said: “The long-term sustainability of the banana sector … cannot be separated from purchasing practices. The price of bananas in the supermarket needs to recognise the real and sustainable cost of production, including labour and decent wages, environmental management, human-rights due diligence and the investment necessary to adapt to climate change.”

Research by the Ethical Trading Initiative found that prices paid to suppliers in 2026 did not even cover basic production costs for 66.7% of them.

It said producers need more help to adapt or mitigate climatic changes, which could mean suitable land for banana cultivation could shrink by an average of 61% across Latin America and the Caribbean by 2050, according to research for the Inter-American Development Bank published in 2020.

#28. Republican congressman to force impeachment vote on Defence Secretary Hegseth

Watch: Moment Thomas Massie introduces articles of impeachment against Pete Hegseth

A Republican congressman plans to force a vote in the House of Representatives this week to impeach Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth, alleging he has violated the US Constitution.

"By engaging in hostilities in Iran for more than 90 days without congressional authorisation, Secretary Hegseth is breaking the law and must be held accountable," Representative Thomas Massie said.

The effort will likely fail, as Republicans remain in power in Congress until January. But Massie says the effort is aimed at pushing President Donald Trump to replace Hegseth before Democrats can launch their own impeachment process.

Hegseth has called the impeachment effort a "sham" and defended his record.

"You see, they attack me. They attack us because we are effective," he said in a video statement last week. "It's not going to work. We will not back down."

He called Massie a "disgruntled, defeated congressman" and said he was not concerned about the impeachment effort. In the video, Hegseth defended his leadership of the department and listed ways he has fulfilled Trump's America First agenda.

Massie formally introduced his articles of impeachment in a resolution on the House floor on Monday afternoon. The move starts a 48-hour clock for the chamber to take up the measure. Lawmakers could vote on the resolution directly, vote to refer it to committee or to set it aside.

"I'm doing this because it's the right thing to do," he told reporters after introducing the articles.

Massie, who lost re-election earlier this year, has been critical of Trump and the war in Iran since it started nearly nine months ago. He has previously garnered headlines over demanding the Trump administration release the files related to deceased sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

The Kentucky congressman first introduced impeachment articles in September and has now brought them back in the wake of Republicans losing control of both the House and Senate in last week's midterm elections.

He has said the effort was aimed at preventing a Democrat spectacle next year when they have control of Congress. He said he hoped to force Trump to name a replacement, who could be quickly confirmed while Republicans have power.

"My effort to impeach Secretary Hegseth is a much gentler version of what's coming when the Democrats assume control of the House and Senate next year," Massie also said in a statement. "They will hold hearings and trials."

Democratic leadership has signalled openness to such an effort against Hegseth once they take control in January.

Asked about the effort on Monday, Hegseth said, "We're not worried about that".

He said he planned to hold a news conference on Tuesday to discuss the subject.

Impeaching a cabinet secretary is rare. It must be approved by both chambers for the secretary to be removed from office.

Trump was impeached by the House twice in his first term in office but was cleared by the Senate after two trials and not removed.

As with the presidential process, the House would first hold a vote on charges against Hegseth and the Senate then would conduct a trial to decide whether to acquit or convict.

The House has voted to impeach only two cabinet members in US history, but the Senate did not convict either one.

While impeachment has been "an incredibly rare process", it has become far more normalised since the House impeached President Bill Clinton in 1998, said Paul Gowder, a law professor at Northwestern University in Chicago.

He said it has become "much more common as the country has become more politically polarised," adding a prediction that several more of Trump's cabinet members may face impeachment over the next two years.

Massie told reporters he was not sure if the measure had sufficient backing to pass. It would need a majority to pass the House and even with all Democratic support, it would still likely need some Republican support as the party holds a slim majority.

Peter Loge, a professor of public affairs at George Washington University, told the BBC that he believed it was likely to pass due to those slim margins.

"There are enough angry Republicans that more than enough Republicans are gonna simply not show up to vote," he said.

Currently, there are 218 Republicans and 214 Democrats in the House.

While the House requires a simple majority to pass articles of impeachment, the Senate mandates a two-thirds vote to convict, threshold that is difficult to meet in the current congressional term.

When Democrats take over in January, they are likely to have at least 54 Senate seats - though two races do not yet have winners due to slim voting margins. A total of 60 votes will be required to convict and remove an official from office.

#27. Pope’s visit to Argentina sets up showdown with ‘hyper-capitalist’ Milei

President has opposing views on many issues, and once called Leo’s predecessor the ‘representative of the Evil One’

Trees are being planted in the garden. The brightly coloured buildings are getting another coat of paint. The choir rehearses. Hundreds of other residents, several of them using wheelchairs, practise their positions outside the sanctuary. Excitement is in the air, because on Monday 9 November, the Cottolengo Don Orione – a Catholic-run home for disabled people – will be the first stop on Pope Leo XIV’s first full day of his trip to Argentina.

But the pope’s visit to Claypole, about 26km from Buenos Aires, does not come at an especially happy time for the home, which counts about 400 residents with disabilities. Argentina’s president, Javier Milei, has made wide-ranging cuts to disability funding.

“[The government] cut off the supply of medications, special foods and diapers. They haven’t delivered any since last November. The matter is in court,” said the facility’s coordinator, Fernando Montero. The pope and Milei, he said, “have completely opposing ideologies”, arguing that the pope teaches that a person’s worth is not measured by whether they are productive, whereas Milei is a “hyper-capitalist” who believes only “those who compete the most should be rewarded”.

Pope Leo’s three days here – he began a three-country trip through South America in Uruguay on Friday, and will finish with six days in Peru – will be the first papal visit to Argentina since John Paul II in 1987. His predecessor, Pope Francis, who was Argentine, never returned during his papacy.

The trip could prove uncomfortable for Milei. A far-right libertarian economist who campaigned with a chainsaw as the symbol of his promised spending cuts, Milei called Francis an “imbecile” and “the representative of the Evil One on Earth” in 2020, and accused him of promoting communism, saying social justice was theft. Milei claims to have apologised when he met Francis in the Vatican in February 2024.

Since taking office the previous December, he has slashed public spending, which the government says was needed to curb inflation. The cost, however, was high. The thinktank Mate estimates that retirees on the minimum pension – 63% of the total – have lost about 30% of their purchasing power. Last Wednesday at the weekly retirees’ march outside Congress, a man held up a cardboard sign reading, “Save us Pope Leo XIV – Milei is killing us.”

Last month, Buenos Aires archbishop Jorge García Cuerva said Leo XIV would find “a divided Argentina and a large number of very poor brothers and sisters.”

But the differences between the pope and the president go beyond typical social issues. In May, Leo XIV called for “disarming” artificial intelligence, while Milei has said he would not regulate AI technology and has instead presented bills to build datacentres across the country and to grant legal status to “no-human corporations”. Federico Sturzenegger, a minister in charge of state cuts, says he has used AI to draft legislation.

Milagros Miceli, a sociologist and computer scientist who leads the Critical AI Lab in Berlin, said the “contradiction” between the two is “pretty evident”. She welcomed Leo’s visit, celebrating how the “powerful figure” of the pope intends to put “human beings at the centre by stripping these technologies of the values that are presented to us as taking precedence over humanity.”

Juan Grabois, a left-wing Peronist deputy and social leader who served in the Vatican’s Dicastery for Promoting Integral Human Development, said: “The idea of absolute deregulation is at the heart of the president’s agenda, and the replacement of the human element with these forms of algorithmic power has clearly also become a policy priority for the government.”

Disability funding has been a flashpoint between church and state. As Milei has clashed with Congress over a funding law that remains in limbo, Argentina’s conference of bishops wrote to the health minister, Mario Lugones, on 1 April, denouncing “the serious situation facing numerous institutions dedicated to the care and support of people with disabilities in our country”. The ministry has met the bishops twice since, in April and again on 5 October, but without resolving the impasse.

The government is trying to downplay potential tensions. “There’s no way the pope is going to come here to comment on local politics,” Sandra Pettovello, the minister overseeing social policy, said recently. Amnesty International, meanwhile, has written to the pope about Argentina, warning of its “concern about government decisions that affect the exercise of fundamental rights”.

All of this will be top of mind when Milei receives the pope at the airport on Sunday. From the disability home, the pope will go on to Buenos Aires and meet business and union leaders; then on Tuesday he will celebrate Mass at the grounds of FAdeA, the state-owned military aircraft factory in Córdoba, whose workers have protested against Milei’s cuts. Juan Cruz Esquivel, director of Buenos Aires University’s Barometer of Religions and Beliefs, says the itinerary speaks for itself.

“I don’t want to speculate [about what the pope will say], but clearly any mention of defending domestic production, labour, disability or social justice in the pope’s rhetoric naturally clashes with the ideology that Milei’s government has been promoting,” says Esquivel.

Mariano Oberlin, a Catholic priest in an impoverished neighbourhood of Córdoba, expects the clash to play out diplomatically. “Pope Leo has been saying it without confronting, but he keeps upholding the church’s social doctrine as the key axis of his ministry,” Oberlin said. “If Milei wants to use him, he’ll probably have to do a lot of juggling to make him say the opposite of what he’s been saying – and of what he’ll surely say when he comes: that the Gospel invites us to feel like brothers, to respect each person’s deep, inherent dignity, and to fight so that this dignity becomes real in access to rights.”

Since Francis’s tenure, social movements in Latin America have come closer to the Catholic church, and Argentina is no exception. Grabois, who was part of that process, sees the relationship as a two-way exchange. “[Francis] wasn’t the ‘good priest’ who came to help the poor,” he says, arguing that the exchanges helped Francis “develop his social thinking”. “Without the cartoneros (cardboard pickers) and the villeros (people from impoverished neighbourhoods), Francis would not have been Francis.”

To Grabois, the same is true for Leo. “Francis took notice of Leo because he saw him supporting the Peruvian indigenous movement.”

That doesn’t mean all Argentine social movements support the pope. On Saturday, a day before the visit, the LGBTQIA+ community held the 35th Pride March in Buenos Aires. María Rachid from the march’s organising committee said: “The church’s dogma regarding our families has not changed at any time, neither under the previous pope nor under this one”, though she notes that Francis’s words on gay rights “brought some relief to many families and their communities”.

But a new development is the rise of evangelical churches, while Catholic identification falls. Buenos Aires University found 57.7% of Argentines identify as Catholic and 17.4% as evangelical, compared with 76.5% and 9% nearly 20 years ago. Last week, Milei inaugurated an evangelical temple in Neuquén and gave a nakedly political speech promising to seek re-election next year. Although Catholicism remains Argentina’s majority religion, it has lost its cultural hegemony and its influence on legislative agenda, Esquivel said.

Nevertheless, a majority of Argentines expect a “message from the pope focused on the social and humanitarian agenda”. Grabois notes the transformative impact that first Francis and now Leo have had “with left-leaning activists” in Argentina. “I’m not speaking about atheists, but outright anticlerical people. People who, 10 years ago, held views that were very, very hostile toward the church,” he said. “I even see them as more fervent [about the visit] than typical churchgoers who attend Sunday Mass.” For Grabois, “in the face of dehumanisation, ordinary people are taking refuge in the only global voice that’s speaking out about this today – which is Francis and Leo’s.”

#23. France accuses Iran of supermarket fridge hacking

Fridges and freezers in hundreds of supermarkets across France were affected last month

France has accused Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) of being behind cyber-attacks that raised the temperatures of supermarket fridges and freezers across the country last month.

Foreign Minister Jean-NoÃ«l Barrot said the attack was "unprecedented in both its scale and its level of aggression", adding those who were responsible would be hit with sanctions.

Iran has yet to respond officially, but some Iranian media outlets have dismissed the allegations as baseless.

The French hacking, which led to the destruction of tons of frozen and chilled food in France was followed by a similar attack against supermarkets across several other European countries. It is not known if the instances are linked.

Barrot said the attack on France's supermarkets "marks the first large-scale cyber-attack aimed at sabotage on our national territory, and it constitutes a major attack on the nation's interests and the security of the French people".

He said the government had summoned the Iranian ambassador to Paris to "lodge a very strong protest against this extremely grave act".

France will take "any other necessary and proportionate measures" to respond to the incident and "deter anyone who might seek to repeat it", Barrot added.

The Paris prosecutor said an investigation had been opened into the head of an offensive cyber unit associated with the IRGC as well as his two deputies.

Laure Beccuau named the unit as Shahid Kaveh, which she said had an affiliation with the electronic and cyber centre of the IRGC.

Shahid Kaveh is the name given to a cluster of hackers thought to be directly employed by the Iranian regime.

In recent weeks, a number of commentators and politicians in Iran have argued that the country should extend any retaliation beyond the Middle East if the US and Israel resume the war they started earlier this year.

If the IRGC carried out the cyber-attacks, it remains unclear whether they were part of Iran's deterrence strategy.

On 11 October, hackers gained control of the software that regulates the temperatures of the refrigeration units used in many of France's supermarkets.

More than 100 of CoopÃ©rative U's supermarkets were hit, as well as 180 stores from IntermarchÃ© and its Netto brand. In some stores, temperatures of the fridges reportedly rose to 50C (120F) overnight.

Other major food retailers including Carrefour, Monoprix and E.Leclerc were also hit by the attack, which targeted software provided by Danish engineering firm Danfoss.

The software allows retailers to manage their cold units remotely.

Earlier this month a second wave of hacks took place, hitting more than 100 stores across Europe.

Supermarkets in Belgium and Luxembourg were affected, while a smaller number of shops were hit in countries including Sweden, Ireland, the Netherlands, Estonia and Finland.

Separately in August a small number of US military bases had fridges mysteriously malfunction, spoiling food. This incident is also being investigated as deliberate cyber-attacks, Reuters news agency has reported, citing sources familiar with the matter.

Since the waves of attacks on freezers, experts have been speculating which hacking groups could be responsible, with Iran being on a list of potential suspects.

In spite of a pair of Russian-aligned hacktivist groups claiming they were responsible on Wednesday, few believed them.

The French government has not said how it reached the conclusion that Iran was responsible, and attribution of cyber-attacks is extremely hard as hackers are able to hide their locations.

Additional reporting by Farshad Bayan, BBC Persian.

#22. Pope Leo delivers stinging rebuke of Milei’s austerity measures during Argentina visit

Pontiff tells Argentina’s president the economy must benefit all on second stop of 12-day Latin America tour

Pope Leo XIV has delivered a stinging critique of Argentine president Javier Milei’s harsh austerity measures, insisting growth must benefit all in a speech met with rapturous applause in Buenos Aires.

“The economy must be at the service of the people, rather than people being at the service of the economy,” he said on Sunday, shortly after landing in Argentina on the second leg of a Latin America tour.

Leo stressed the plight of those who, “despite hard work, nonetheless struggle to meet their household’s needs”.

He spoke out in defence of the poor and pensioners, insisting that “particular concern be shown for those affected by illness, dependency and disability” – a veiled reference to the government’s controversial cuts to disability assistance.

He also told Milei, known for his foul-mouthed diatribes, that strengthening democracy required a “willingness to listen” and the “careful use of words”.

Leo, 71, is on an ambitious 12-day tour of the continent, just three weeks after undergoing surgery for lung cancer.

The visit is the first by a pontiff to Argentina in nearly four decades, and Leo was met with calls to help mend deep divisions in the country after three years of austerity under Milei.

Argentina is the home country of the late pope, Francis, who Milei famously insulted for his pro-poor teachings.

Leo said he was “retracing” Francis’s steps.

Argentines are hoping the leader of the world’s 1.4 billion Catholics will help unite the country, which is bitterly divided between supporters and opponents of Milei.

“Just like the World Cup unites us, hopefully the pope will too,” said Liliana Deblauwe, a 68-year-old retired teacher who waited with hundreds of people to see the pope visit a monument to liberation hero Jose de San Martin.

Hundreds more chanting “Papa Leon” (Spanish for Leo) gathered outside the presidential palace, where he was hosted by Milei.

Brian Inacio Rodriguez, a 26-year-old job seeker, spent five days travelling overland from Ushuaia, at the very tip of South America, to see the pontiff.

He said he hoped he brought “much peace to Argentina”.

Leo arrived in Buenos Aires from Uruguay, where he began his first trip as pope to Latin America, the world’s most Catholic continent which is becoming increasingly secular.

In Uruguay, he condemned drug violence and urged an end to child poverty.

In Buenos Aires, he insisted on the importance of “personal integrity” in public office, stressing that “corruption undermines the confidence of citizens in their institutions.”

The Argentine stage of his visit aims to boost the flagging faith of a country left disappointed by Francis’s failure to visit during his papacy.

It will also act as a litmus test of support for the church. In a recent University of Buenos Aires poll, 57.7% of people identified as Catholic, down from about 90% in the 1960s.

Leo will visit a home for disabled people and a prison and celebrate three open-air masses.

The church has said it expects 7 million to attend the various events.

Milei came to office on a promise to revive Argentina’s ailing economy and tame runaway inflation by shrinking the welfare state, targeting public hospitals, universities and pensioners in particular.

The poverty rate has swung wildly since he came to power – increasing to 32.3% in September.

A close ally of US president Donald Trump, with a similar combative style, Milei had a tense relationship with Francis.

On the campaign trail the cost-cutting economist called his countryman an “imbecile” for championing social justice.

Once in office, however, he softened his tone, but he continues to heap insults on the press and opposition.

His relationship with Leo has so far been cordial, but the local Catholic church has heavily criticised him for treating key social services solely as a budgetary constraint.

Milei has also been criticised for restricting several democratic freedoms, including the right to protest. But he maintains a loyal supporter base for drastically reducing inflation.

#19. Deadly floods hit Iraq as thunderstorms sweep across region

Violent storms lash northern Iraq and parts of Iran, while Portugal faces strong winds and Japan braces for further snowfall

Deadly flash floods hit northern Iraq on Saturday as severe thunderstorms swept across the region, including in neighbouring Iran. The city of Erbil, in Iraq, was particularly badly hit, with extensive damage to homes and businesses. Streets were inundated as torrential rain fell over a short period.

Rainfall exceeded forecasts, with an estimated 80mm falling in the city, compared with expectations of 30mm. Two people were killed in the floods.

In Europe, Storm Bruna is forecast to bring strong winds to Portugal this week, particularly along the west coast. Gusts are expected to reach 50mph (80km/h), with heavy rain and thunderstorms also expected. The total rainfall could reach 200mm by the end of the week. The storm has already affected the Canary Islands, where Tenerife recorded a gust of 80mph on Saturday night.

Japan experienced a cold and snowy end to last week, with further snowfall expected in Hokkaido and parts of the mountains in the main island Honshu.

Temperatures in Japan are forecast to fall below the seasonal average early this week, with Sapporo, the capital of Hokkaido prefecture, expected to plunge to 10C below average. Maximum temperatures in the city are likely to be about 4-6C on Monday and Wednesday, while Tuesday’s high is forecast to be about 0C. Minimum temperatures could fall to -6C by Wednesday, and -8C in other western parts of Hokkaido on Tuesday.

Ski resorts are preparing for visitors after snowfall began on Friday. Total snowfall is expected to reach 30-50cm around Kiroro and Niesko, with about 20-30cm in Furano, Rusutsu and Sapporo.

Other resorts could receive up to 20cm of snow by Wednesday. Strong winds accompanied the snow on Friday and over the weekend, with gusts of about 75mph in Kiroko and Niesko.

During the strong El Niño, sea surface temperatures in the equatorial Pacific were about 3.5C above the climatological average at their peak in late October. As a result, Peru has been experiencing air temperatures 5-10C above the seasonal average. The unusually warm conditions expected to continue into next week.

#18. Epstein associate who still runs model agency 'put me in danger' says abuse survivor

Roza has decided to share her story with the BBC

A woman raped by Jeffrey Epstein says the president of the model agency representing her put her in danger, when he sent her to two photoshoots at the convicted sex offender's Florida beach house.

At the time, in 2009, Epstein was serving a jail sentence for sex offences against a minor but was allowed to work each day from his home.

"My agency's job was to protect me, and they failed. In fact, they put me in danger. And for that, they should be held accountable," she tells the BBC World Service.

Roza was 18 when she was first sent to Epstein's Palm Beach home by MC2 Model Management's US branch, having only arrived in the US from Uzbekistan about a month earlier. MC2's US president at the time, Jeff Fuller, now runs another US model agency called The Source Models.

"My agency knew exactly where I was. So Jeff knew exactly where I was going," says Roza.

Jeff Fuller now runs The Source Models agency

She shows us an expense statement suggesting Fuller himself booked her flight home from Palm Beach, following her second shoot at Epstein's home. Fuller has previously claimed he did not know Roza was going to Epstein's house.

She says the photoshoots led to her being employed as an assistant to Epstein. Other survivors have explained how the financier used the recruitment of assistants as an elaborate ruse to allow him to continue abusing young women.

Roza says Epstein went on to systematically rape her.

Fuller has not responded to the BBC's allegations. He has previously said he did not know Roza was being abused.

We discovered through the Epstein-related documents released by the US Department of Justice [DOJ] that 10 models from MC2 met, or were in communication with, Epstein after his conviction.

The files also show Epstein gave $1m (Â£742,000) to MC2 in 2005 - despite the agency owners telling a US publication two years later that Epstein had no involvement in their company.

Testimony shared with BBC Eye also raises questions about the role played by two other individuals who were active in the model industry, and whom the DOJ files show had close relationships with Epstein - former agent Ramsey Elkholy and agency director Faith Kates.

A DOJ statement from last year, that no evidence has been uncovered which would warrant further investigation against "uncharged third parties", is now being challenged by New York State senator, Zellnor Myrie.

He has proposed a new sex trafficking bill to give police greater power to investigate Epstein's alleged enablers.

Senator Myrie told the BBC he wants those who knew - or should have known - about Epstein's abuse to be held accountable

Sara Ziff, founder of the Model Alliance, which campaigns for stronger protections for models in the US, approves.

"Often, [model agencies] are the problem. They are the link between the model and these predatory men."

Roza says the person behind the camera at the photoshoots MC2 sent her to was not a professional photographer, but Epstein's girlfriend at the time, Nadia Marcinko.

Roza describes the set-up as "strange - there was no stylist" and the photos were never used. She has since become convinced the shoots were stage-managed by Epstein and MC2.

BBC Eye reveals new evidence showing how scouts, agencies and powerful fashion figures helped Jeffrey Epstein gain access to young women after his conviction for sex offences.

If you are outside the UK, please watch on YouTube, external

An email in the DOJ files, from Fuller to Epstein, makes clear the close ties between the agency and the financier.

"Had it not been for you, we would have fired her months ago," Fuller writes of another model in March 2012. "And I would be careful as she blabs about things," he says.

A month after Epstein's second arrest in 2019, MC2's US branch was dissolved and Fuller opened a new agency, The Source Models, which is still operating. The BBC has found that 50 models from MC2 were transferred to his new agency.

We asked The Source Models to comment on our findings - it did not reply.

Model agencies were useful to Epstein, says Sigrid McCawley, the lead lawyer for more than 100 of Epstein's victims.

"Epstein said to many people he wanted three different girls a day. Think about those numbers... The [model] scouts were really critical because they were the ones who were identifying the type of woman that Epstein liked."

In the case of former agent Ramsey Elkholy, BBC Eye has discovered he tried to connect 34 women to Epstein, including an 18-year-old who was still in school.

The experience of one of the women highlights how assistants would be co-opted into the structure of the abuse.

Elkholy, the DOJ files show, suggested to Epstein that one of the financier's female assistants befriend this woman, before introducing her to Epstein.

We spoke to this woman, and she revealed that at her first meeting with Epstein, aged 19, he sexually abused her in his New York townhouse.

Ramsey Elkholy told the BBC: "I completely reject any allegation that I knew of or facilitated abuse of any kindâ¦ At the time, I had no knowledge of any abuse and I knew only his own [Epstein's] account of his 2008 [legal] case, which I misguidedly believed."

He continued: "The record and published reporting show that women were often groomed by other women in his inner circle. I was never part of that circle, or indeed of any such activityâ¦ my intention was to help models find professional opportunities by introducing them to an extremely well-connected individual."

If you are in the UK and you have been affected by any of the issues in this story such as sexual abuse and violence, information and support is available at BBC Action Line

Lawyer Sigrid McCawley says these female assistants would have had "no choice" in how they conducted themselves.

"These young survivors were being abused regularly. Their livelihoods were being threatenedâ¦ They had to do what they were being told to do."

Another model we have spoken to, Simona (not her real name), from eastern Europe, told us Epstein had raped her more than 200 times.

"People think that we gained something, that we agreed on this abuse in order to gain something, whether it's monetary or influential, but it's not true. We're left with deep trauma," says Simona.

She says the first time she met the financier, aged 20, she was already signed with a model agency in New York, and he offered to introduce her to his "good" friend Faith Kates.

At the time, Kates was the head of Next Management, one of the world's biggest model agencies. She stepped down from that senior executive position in December last year, but is still legally listed as a director of the company.

Simona says Faith Kates, pictured here, met her at Epstein's request

Simona says at this meeting, Kates suggested she undergo plastic surgery and sent details of a surgeon to Epstein.

After her meeting with Kates, Simona says Epstein convinced her to stop modelling.

And just like Roza, and several other of his victims, she became his assistant. She says he went on to rape her and control every aspect of her life for nearly two years.

The DOJ files show Epstein asked Kates to meet an additional five models, though it is not clear how many of these meetings took place.

Lawyers for Faith Kates told the BBC: "Jeffrey Epstein was a master manipulator... Ms Kates was one of those grossly misled by Epstein. Had she known then what she - and everyone else - now knows, she would have never associated with him."

They added she had never asked a model to have plastic surgery.

We approached Next Management for comment, but it did not reply.

Myrie told the BBC that if his bill were to become law, and "you were sending models to Epstein and you knew what Epstein was doing, or you should have known what he was doing, you will be held accountable".

Roza hopes sharing her story will encourage others to come forward.

"There are many, many survivors out there who never speak, because it's scary. So to all my sister survivorsâ¦ you know me now. You know my name. I say, reach out."

#13. Ukraine peace talks resume with 'some flexibility' by warring sides, US officials say

The talks come as Ukraine reels from a deadly Russian strike on a village in Zaporizhzhia last week, in which at least 40 people were killed

Moscow and Kyiv are privately showing "a little bit of flexibility" on the issue of who would controls Ukraine's eastern regions in a postwar scenario, US officials have said.

The comments came on the eve of the first trilateral talks aimed at ending the war in Ukraine since February.

Senior officials from Moscow, Kyiv and Washington will meet in Abu Dhabi as the fifth anniversary of Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine approaches.

Jared Kushner - Donald Trump's son-in-law and part of the US delegation alongside peace envoy Steve Witkoff - said the talks would be focused on addressing the "postwar security concerns" of both Russia and Ukraine.

"An important part of this process is encouraging all parties to begin thinking seriously about what comes after the war," Kushner wrote on X.

But Moscow and Kyiv's positions on ending the conflict in the first place remain far apart.

A key area of disagreement between the two involves Ukraine's eastern region of Donetsk, which is by now mostly occupied by Russia.

Moscow has demanded that Ukraine relinquish the parts of Donetsk which it still holds, while Kyiv wants to freeze the contact line to maintain control over those sections, which it says are crucial to its future security.

While neither side has budged on these demands, the US official speaking on the eve of the talks suggested that this was just the public stance and that Moscow and Kyiv had shown "a little bit of flexibility, privately".

However, he acknowledged that the issue would not be solved without discussions about a new "security architecture".

"They will not solve the Donetsk issue and the territorial issue until they know what the security architecture is going to be after the war," the official said. "And keep in mind, we're not going to be able to finalise one without the other."

For Ukraine, a post-war "security architecture" may include reassurance that allies will come to its aid if it's attacked again. For Russia, that could be its longstanding ambition to see Nato roll back from eastern Europe â which would be alarming to Europeans.

The framework being proposed includes security consultations and crisis communications hotlines, as well as "reciprocal de-escalation measures" between the two. The officials also floated the re-activation of the Nato-Russia Council that has been largely paused since 2022.

Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff are part of the US delegation that will attend the talks in Abu Dhabi

The last round of trilateral talks, held in Geneva in February, ended without a breakthrough. Soon after, the US-Israel war on Iran diverted Washington's attention from Ukraine, and no other discussions were held since.

Since then, European leaders - who have been largely excluded from peace talks â have pushed to be involved in the process amid concerns that decisions about the security of their continent would be made without them.

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen urged on Monday to include Nato allies and EU members into any "meaningful discussions about Europe's security architecture". President Volodymyr Zelensky has repeatedly stated Kyiv wanted Europe to be part of the talks.

The US official said the Americans would propose moving into a multilateral format bringing the Europeans into the discussion. "Hopefully we'll get the Russians on board," the official said, while acknowledging that "the Russians have a bad taste in their mouth with the Europeans."

Since the start of his second term in office, US President Donald Trump â who once boasted he could end the war in Ukraine within a day â has spearheaded several rounds of talks to end the conflict, albeit with little success.

Meanwhile, his increasingly conciliatory approach towards Moscow has fuelled deep unease in Kyiv and across the European Union.

After meeting Kremlin envoy Kirill Dmitriev in Washington last week, Trump touted a forthcoming "big, beautiful agreement" on Ukraine which "might involve all of Europe".

Many in Ukraine are sceptical that this would result into a fair peace.

Ukrainian MP Oleksandr Merezhko, head of the foreign affairs parliamentary committee, told the BBC he believes Trump wants "a quick deal at the expense of Ukraine", which will certainly be rejected by Kyiv.

The only way to make Putin start real negotiations is by putting greater pressure on Russia through sanctions and deep strikes, Merezhko said.

"While Putin has a war machine up and running, he has no incentives to negotiate." He also mentioned recent moves by Trump, such as his deal to buy diesel from Russia, as something that "only encourages Moscow to continue the war".

Moscow's delegation in Abu Dhabi will include Kremlin aide Vladimir Medinsky, deputy foreign ministers Sergei Ryabkov and Mikhail Galuzin, spy chief Igor Kostyukov and army general Yury Zhigarlovsky.

Kyiv is sending several senior officials who have been part of previous negotiating teams, including Foreign Intelligence Service head Rustem Umerov, Volodymyr Zelensky's chief of staff Kyrylo Budanov, and MP Davyd Arakhamiya.

Away from Abu Dhabi, Russia continues to pummel Ukraine with deadly bombardments and strikes on energy infrastructure, viewed in Kyiv as proof Moscow wants to corner Ukraine into accepting a deal on Russia's terms.

On Monday, Kushner was cautious to point out that the Abu Dhabi talks could at most "build momentum toward the next phase of negotiations."

"This is an extraordinarily difficult and complicated conflict," he wrote on X, adding the US was "approaching these discussions with determination and realism."

#12. British healthcare worker with Ebola evacuated from DR Congo to UK

Patient is ⁠in ​stable condition and will be transferred to a specialist ‘high consequence’ infectious disease unit

A British healthcare worker with Ebola is being evacuated from the Democratic Republic of the Congo to the UK for medical treatment, it has been confirmed.

“A British citizen currently in the DR Congo has tested positive for Ebola,” a spokesperson for the UK government said in a statement on Wednesday. “The UK has well-established procedures for dealing with cases like this, and those processes have been activated … The risk to the UK public from Ebola remains low.”

The patient, whose identity is unknown, is in a stable condition and is expected to arrive in the UK on Wednesday or Thursday before being transferred in a specialist ambulance to a “high consequence” infectious disease unit, according to an official document seen by Reuters.

A humanitarian organisation sent the worker to DR Congo to help in the response to the outbreak, which is the second largest on record. It is the first case involving a British national contracting Ebola in the current outbreak.

The Royal Free hospital in London has a high-level isolation unit for infectious diseases such as Ebola; other centres are in Newcastle and Liverpool.

Ebola is a highly contagious, severe and often fatal illness. It is spread through direct contact with body fluids such as the blood or vomit of infected people, or dead bodies.

The current outbreak, declared on 15 May by DR Congo’s health ministry, has surpassed 10,000 cases, and 5,000 deaths. It is the 17th in DR Congo since Ebola was identified, was caused by the Bundibugyo virus, and has become the largest and deadliest in the country’s history. The Bundibugyo virus has no approved treatment, though vaccine trials are under way.

Since the outbreak started, it has spread to Kenya and Uganda in east Africa, while last month Nigeria confirmed a case in the capital, Abuja, after a woman who arrived from Uganda tested positive for the virus. She, along with a close contact, were evacuated to Switzerland for treatment.

In an interview with the Guardian last month, Dr Jean Kaseya, the director general of the Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), said the “unprecedented” spread of the Ebola outbreak meant it could reach the UK and other countries.

“We want all countries to know that they are not safe if they don’t take appropriate measures,” he said. “Each country, not only in the African region, but all continents, must now increase the preparedness level and the possibility for treatment.

“It is the first time three African regions have been affected [by Ebola]. The geographic extension of this outbreak is unprecedented.”

Other patients who have been medically evacuated for specialised care since the start of the outbreak include two sent to Germany and one to the Netherlands. Another case was reported in France in July.

British health authorities say the risk ⁠to the wider public from imported Ebola cases remains low and the NHS has procedures in place to manage suspected and confirmed infections.

There have only been four confirmed cases recorded in Britain since 1976, ‌according to the UK Health Security Agency.

#9. Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Hundreds of billions of dollars have been wiped off the value of Nvidia after the US chipmaker announced an independent investigation would be carried out into its chief executive's affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

The tech firm saw its shares fall by about 4% by close of trade on Monday after Jensen Huang admitted having an extramarital affair.

Nvidia's probe came after reports that the US believed the Nvidia boss had been targeted in an espionage campaign by China.

However, China has rejected the claims, with its foreign ministry saying allegations about Chinese spies were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Nvidia said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised, but a statement by the firm's board of directors said it would begin an investigation in "light of recent reporting that founder and chief executive Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report, external.

The report said Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington last spring warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Jensen Huang admitted he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children. He added he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation.

The Nvidia co-founder has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week.

Members of the US House Foreign Affairs Committee last week asked Jensen Huang to appear for a closed-door briefing on his interactions with Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's security protocols around espionage.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's [China's] access to sensitive US technology and information," congressmen Brian Mast, John Moolenaar, and Rick Crawford wrote in a letter.

"It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which US national security has been placed at risk."

After Nvidia's investigation was announced, other big tech bosses, including Elon Musk, came out in support of Jensen Huang to continue leading the firm.

US President Donald Trump said the Nvidia boss was an "honourable guy".

However, the news sparked a sell-off in Nvidia's shares, with about $250bn (Â£190bn) wiped of the company's market value.

While Nvidia shares fell, the US stock market opened the week on a positive note after last week's turmoil, with Nvidia's rival chipmakers benefiting.

But analysts suggested the hit to the company would be short-lived. Victoria Fernandez, chief market strategist at Crossmark Global Investments said investors would not abandon Nvidia, because the chipmaker was so important to the AI industry.

"Nvidia is the core of this AI trade, and without them, all these other pieces start to fall apart because the financing's not there," she told the BBC.

Jensen Huang co-founded Nvidia, which is central to making the hardware for emerging AI technologies, with two other engineers in 1993.

Its chips were initially designed for gaming, but he decided to turn its focus on AI development, turning it into the world's second most valuable company.

A key concern for investors going forward is what would happen to Nvidia if Jensen Huang was removed from the company, as Fernandez said there was "no natural successor" to the chief executive.

Jensen Huang, unlike other founders in the tech industry, is vulnerable to any move by Nvidia's board of directors to oust him.

He owns only around 3.5% of the company, and Nvidia operates on a single class voting structure, meaning his stake would not be enough to act as a veto power over any board decision.

By contrast, many tech founders who lead their companies, including Mark Zuckerberg of Meta and Musk of Tesla and SpaceX, hold significant percentages of multiple types of stock in their companies.

Fernandez said she does not believe the board will remove Jensen Huang as chief executive, unless there is glaring evidence of wrongdoing related to national security or intellectual property, "but I bet they start bringing someone up as his number two, to have there for the future."

The modest fall in its shares mean investors believe it is unlikely he will step down or be removed as chief executive, but that the outcome of the investigation will be closely watched.

Even people who do not directly own shares in Nvidia can be affected by rises and falls in its value. Pensions or savings invested in pots which have stakes in the company through index tracking funds can be impacted, for example.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia chips have been a major focus of the AI race between the US and China and the White House has previously imposed controls on where its advanced products can be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted by Washington over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Jensen Huang has been against restricting the sale of Nvidia's chips to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Additional reporter by Samira Hussain, North America business correspondent.

#8. ANC faces bruising defeat in South African local elections with 80% of ballots counted

Former liberation movement’s share of national vote almost 14 percentage points below 2021 result

The African National Congress is on course to suffer a bruising defeat in South Africa’s local elections, with the once dominant party of Nelson Mandela falling to under a third of the vote nationally with more than 80% of ballots counted.

Wednesday’s fragmented vote marks another stage in the decline of the ANC, which has led national governments since the end of white minority apartheid rule in 1994. High unemployment, crime, corruption and poor local service delivery have all contributed to voters turning away from the party.

The former liberation movement is projected to lose its status as the biggest party in the two most populous provinces, Gauteng and KwaZulu-Natal, as parties began coalition negotiations behind closed doors. The final results are expected to be announced on Sunday.

The ANC was on 31.8% of the vote nationally, almost 14 percentage points below its 2021 local election result. The centre-right, white-led Democratic Alliance (DA) had 24.1% of the vote, while the uMkhonto weSizwe (MK) party of the former ANC president Jacob Zuma was on 9.3%.

Turnout also rose in Wednesday’s poll. The election commission estimated that 54.5% of registered voters cast their ballot, up from 46% five years ago.

The final shape of coalitions in some of the country’s biggest cities remained unclear with the final votes still being counted. Senior figures in key parties said they would talk with almost anyone, while keeping negotiating strategies under wraps.

In Johannesburg, South Africa’s commercial capital and biggest city, with 4.8 million people, voters have had to contend with regular water and power cuts, uncollected rubbish and potholes. The municipality, which has seen 10 changes of mayor in the last decade amid unstable coalitions, has admitted to a 220bn rand (£9.9bn) infrastructure spending backlog.

With just over half of votes counted in Johannesburg, the DA was leading with 40%, having run an energetic campaign under Helen Zille, a 75-year-old former Cape Town mayor. The ANC, which leads the current coalition, was in second place on 17.5%. ActionSA, a rightwing party led by Herman Mashaba, was on 15.5%. Mashaba has pledged never to work with Zille, whom he calls “racist”, something she vehemently denies.

Many of the ANC’s township strongholds have yet to report full results. The Council for Scientific and Industrial Research (CSIR), a respected thinktank, has projected the DA will finish with 34%, the ANC 20% and ActionSA 16.8%.

Coalitions in Gauteng’s three city municipalities are often negotiated together. The DA is leading in all three.

Nesrine Malik, Micha Frazer-Carroll and the team guide you through the biggest Black stories from around the world

The MK party, led by 84-year-old Zuma as he continues to weather and deny numerous corruption allegations, is expected to displace the ANC as the largest party in KwaZulu-Natal. Founded in 2023, it mixes socially conservative Zulu nationalism with pledges to redistribute land and wealth from white to black South Africans.

With half of votes counted in eThekwini municipality, which contains Durban, the MK had 41%, meaning other parties could form a coalition to keep it out of power. Meanwhile, the DA is projected to maintain its majority in Cape Town, despite growing middle-class anger over rising housing costs.

Voters choose a local councillor and a party, with seats allocated via proportional representation. Councillors will then have two weeks to elect mayors and form municipal governments.

#7. Trump pleads not guilty to 34 felony counts

Our live coverage of Trump’s historic indictment has moved.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan wrote his Monday night order that the media’s request to broadcast the arraignment of former President Donald Trump was understandable, but that the news organizations’ interests in providing the broadest possible access to the proceedings must be weighed against “competing interests.”

In rejecting the request to broadcast the arraignment live on television, Merchan still wrote about the historical significance of the proceeding in stark terms.

“That this indictment involves a matter of monumental significance cannot possibly be disputed. Never in the history of the United States has a sitting or past President been indicted on criminal charges. Mr. Trump’s arraignment has generated unparalleled public interest and media attention,” he wrote. “The populace rightly hungers for the most accurate and current information available. To suggest otherwise would be disingenuous.”

News outlets will not be allowed to broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday in a New York state court, a judge said Monday night, but he will allow some photographers to take pictures in the courtroom before the proceedings formally begin.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan rejected the request by several media organizations, including CNN, for permission to broadcast the historic proceedings. Trump’s arraignment — like most arraignments in the Manhattan courthouse — is a public proceeding, but news cameras are not usually allowed to broadcast from inside the courtroom.

However, the judge is allowing five pool photographers to take still photos at the beginning of the proceedings “until such time as they are directed to vacate the jury box by court personnel.”

Earlier on Monday, Trump’s lawyers urged the judge to reject the media’s request for live cameras in the courtroom. The Manhattan District Attorney’s office told the judge they didn’t have a position.

The media outlets that tried to get cameras into the courtroom argued that “the gravity of this proceeding … and, consequently, the need for the broadest possible public access, cannot be overstated.”

Trump is now in Manhattan ahead of the arraignment. A grand jury indicted the former president last week.

The arraignment is also expected to bring the unsealing of the criminal charges against Trump, which have not yet been seen by his lawyers or the public.

The indictment stems from Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg’s investigation into hush-money payments, made during the 2016 presidential campaign, to women who claimed they had extramarital affairs with Trump, which he denies.

Trump denies all wrongdoing and his lawyers said last week that they’ll fight to get the charges dropped.

Donald Trump is not planning to speak publicly in New York on Tuesday, aides say, if he follows advice from members of his legal team who have urged the former President to refrain from the vitriol he’s been directing toward the prosecutor and judge presiding over the case.

But that restraint does not extend to Florida, aides said, where he is planning to deliver a full-throated defense of his New York case as well as other investigations he is facing during a prime time address from Mar-a-Lago on Tuesday night.

It’s the first test to see whether Trump can navigate two vasty different approaches to his legal case and his political campaign, which are now inextricably linked.

“He’s taking this very seriously and following legal advice,” an adviser to Trump said Monday night. “He will make his case to Americans Tuesday night at Mar-a-Lago.”

Alina Habba, who represents Donald Trump in several civil matters, spent time with the former president in New York today and said, “He’s in good spirits. Honestly, he’s as he normally would be. He’s ready to go in and do what he needs to do tomorrow.”

Habba — appearing on on Fox News’ “Jesse Watters” — was asked about the “game plan” for Trump’s appearance in court in Manhattan Tuesday and said, “It’s all mapped out.”

She added, “Barring any surprises, I think that it should be smooth. We’re trying to coordinate and cooperate with everybody to make sure that there are no problems.”

On whether Trump can get a fair trial in Manhattan, Habba said, “No, no. I think it’s very difficult. I’d like to have faith in this state, but I’ve been practicing for him now for a couple years and gone to court in New York for a few years, and I can tell you, it’s not the same as representing anybody else.”

Former President Donald Trump met with his attorneys Susan Necheles and Joe Tacopina at Trump Tower after arriving in New York City Monday, a source familiar with the meeting told CNN.

It is unclear if the newest addition to Trump’s New York legal team, Todd Blanche, attended the meeting.

Trump is expected to be arraigned Tuesday.

History was made Thursday when he was indicted by a New York grand jury. It’s the first time that a current or former US president has been criminally charged.
